#5836. China Doesn’t Want a Nuclear Arms Race

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

LYLE J. GOLDSTEIN is Director of the Asia Program at Defense Priorities and Director of the China Initiative at Brown University’s Watson School of International and Public Affairs.

NINA TANNENWALD is Associate Teaching Professor of Political Science at Brown University.

How America Can Avert an Unnecessary Rivalry

Today, pessimism—almost fatalism—reigns in Washington about the United States’ ability to avoid a costly and dangerous arms race with China. Policymakers point to China’s increasingly aggressive policies toward Taiwan, its massive military buildup, and its resistance to engaging in some arms control measures as evidence that Beijing has accepted the inevitability of such a race or is even pursuing one. Worried by intellectual frameworks that emphasize the likelihood of war between rival great powers, many U.S. leaders and analysts see little choice but for the United States to arm itself, too. But this thinking risks becoming a self-fulling prophecy. The major danger is that an unrestrained arms race increases the likelihood of war over Taiwan or even of a catastrophic nuclear exchange.

But the pessimism about averting an arms race is misplaced. A nuclear arms race, in particular, is not a foregone conclusion—and the United States has real opportunities to curb one. Avoiding a nuclear arms race will require a broader approach than the two-pronged one Washington has recently favored: building up U.S. nuclear deterrence and seeking to force concessions from Beijing by imposing restrictions on trade and technology transfers. American leaders should instead look beyond traditional deterrence, which falls short because its threat-based approach tends to provoke competition over arms, and strive to establish a more fundamental strategic stability through a process of reciprocal de-escalation and compromise. China is likely to be amenable to reciprocal de-escalation: it has a long tradition of moderation in nuclear matters and, ultimately, Chinese leaders do not want a war over Taiwan.

There are recent signs that even U.S. President Donald Trump, who has often boasted about his distaste for compromise, sees the value of engagement. After two U.S.-Chinese bilateral summits this year, in October he suggested that his November trilateral meeting in Shenzhen with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin should focus on “denuclearization.” But Washington will have to become much more consistent in its rejection of an arms race, including by modifying its support for Taiwan and incorporating China into global arms control leadership. A nuclear arms race with China becomes inevitable only if Washington continues to believe it is.

China is undoubtedly pursuing a military buildup, including investments in nuclear weapons. Between 2020 and 2025, China doubled the number of its nuclear warheads, from 300 to 600—a startling increase, although its arsenal remains small compared with the United States’ approximately 3,700 warheads. In 2021, researchers at the Federation of American Scientists announced that China was constructing 350 new nuclear missile silos in three vast sites in Xinjiang and Gansu provinces. Beijing is also pursuing substantial qualitative upgrades to its nuclear weapons, a push it showcased at an October 2025 military parade that unveiled a new sea-launched ballistic missile, two new kinds of intercontinental ballistic missiles, a new air-launched hypersonic missile, and a new long-range hypersonic cruise missile.

Then, this July, the Chinese navy tested a submarine-launched long-range ballistic missile that could carry nuclear weapons. This significant new capability will allow China to launch long-range nuclear strikes that can reach the United States from protected waters near its own coastline rather than having to sail out into the Pacific. A recent editorial in a leading Chinese foreign affairs newspaper took the candid yet disturbing step of explicitly linking China’s rapid nuclear buildup to its desire to exert sovereignty over Taiwan. “The great cause of national reunification,” it asserted, “must rest on a solid, realistic foundation. That foundation is, first and foremost, hard power.” Although such overt nuclear saber rattling remains rare, the link between Beijing’s nuclear forces and the Taiwan issue stretches back over three decades and is also supported by the scholar Tong Zhao’s detailed analysis of China’s nuclear buildup in Foreign Affairs in May.

As China builds up its military, the perception that it is on a collision course with the United States over Taiwan—one that inevitably includes the risk of nuclear escalation—has become widespread in Washington. Taiwan, of course, appears to be an obvious flash point. The United States has long committed itself informally to defending the island while China seeks to unify with it, by force if necessary. In October, Army General Ronald Clark, the head of the U.S. Pacific Command, affirmed that his forces are actively readying for a Chinese invasion of Taiwan that could come as early as 2027.

The Biden administration and both Trump administrations made some attempts to engage China over arms control and nuclear risk reduction. Trump proposed trilateral arms control talks that would include Russia, whereas the Biden administration pursued bilateral talks with China on strategic stability and nuclear risk reduction. But both presidents gave up after China consistently rejected engaging in sustained talks on nuclear matters. Beijing’s stance is that it has a small nuclear arsenal compared with the United States and Russia and that it is important to first establish trust and mutual reassurance about core interests.

Beyond China’s resistance to arms control, theories about power that are trendy in Washington reinforce the collision course mentality. Numerous realist scholars argue that a U.S.-Chinese rivalry and even a great-power war may be structurally difficult to avoid. The political scientist Graham Allison captured this pessimism in his influential 2017 book, Destined for War, which argued that a so-called Thucydides trap—when a rapidly rising power challenges the position of the dominant power, yielding war—would doom the United States and China to conflict.

China hawks, such as Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby, increasingly advocate an aggressive focus on “preparing for World War III” with China as a way to avoid it. In a July New York Times interview, Colby argued that the United States must “deny” China “the ability to seize and hold the key territory” of Taiwan, by enhancing U.S. deterrence with a massive military buildup. The next month, speaking at a military conference, Colby specified that such a buildup ought to put a greater emphasis on tactical nuclear weapons and the ability to fight a so-called limited nuclear war. The enemy, Colby said, has “got to believe there’s some sense in which we would use” nuclear weapons. Toward this end, the United States is building a new intercontinental ballistic missile, a new air-launched cruise missile, and new nuclear-armed submarines and bombers, and accelerating the ambitious Golden Dome homeland missile defense project. Unlike earlier U.S. missile defenses, which were designed to intercept limited strikes by so called rogue states such as Iran and North Korea, the Golden Dome is intended to defend against massive Chinese or Russian attacks. But the prospect of losing its ability to retaliate against a U.S. first strike only gives Beijing more incentive to expand its nuclear arsenal.

Such a nuclear-heavy approach only prevents a war with China if nuclear deterrence succeeds. That puts a lot of eggs in the deterrence basket. And recent wars suggest that nuclear deterrence is becoming less effective. Russia’s nuclear threats have not coerced Ukraine into halting its strikes deep into Russian territory. Iran has proved willing to launch strikes against nuclear-armed Israel as well as U.S. bases and allies in the Middle East. What is most ominous, in May 2025, India and Pakistan crossed previous redlines and attacked each other’s homelands, something that mutual nuclear deterrence was supposed to prevent. Even if neither the United States nor China want nuclear war, a China determined to unify with Taiwan may not be intimidated by U.S. nuclear threats.

A recent study by Brown University’s Costs of War program found that the United States has already spent more than $3.4 trillion on its militarized rivalry with China over the last decade. Sustaining or ramping up this spending—which encompasses new warships, aircraft, long-range missiles, drones, base upgrades, forward deployments, large military exercises, research and development, and new nuclear weapons—is infeasible in an era of ballooning national debt and expanding domestic needs.

One of the tragedies of an arms race is that neither party may actually want to pursue one. Despite its current arms buildup, Beijing has a long record of moderation in military and nuclear matters. For many decades, it has maintained a minimal nuclear deterrent and a no-first-use policy, and it did not use major military force to achieve strategic goals. Although it has long had access to the relevant technologies, China appears not to have produced or deployed tactical nuclear weapons. Chinese diplomats played a substantial role in coaxing Iran to accept the Obama administration’s 2015 nuclear deal and supported the UN’s relatively stringent sanctions on North Korea. And in the fall of 2022, according to then Secretary of State Antony Blinken and reporting by the Financial Times, China likely intervened diplomatically with the Kremlin to help stop Russia from using nuclear weapons in Ukraine. More broadly, China has embraced international law when it comes to war, playing a constructive role, for example, in removing chemical weapons from Syria in 2013.

Beijing’s inclination toward restraint has not disappeared completely, even as it upgrades its military. In November 2023, U.S. President Joe Biden and Xi agreed to renew lines of communication between their countries’ militaries that had been severed after U.S. House Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taiwan the previous year. A year later, the two leaders affirmed that humans, not AI, must control the decision to use nuclear weapons. And in late 2025, Beijing released a white paper emphatically reiterating its support for nuclear arms control and has moved toward more transparency by publishing more information, including in English, about its military upgrades.

Chinese leaders clearly do not want a war over Taiwan. Publicly, they repeatedly emphasize that their goal is “peaceful unification” and “winning without fighting,” invoking the Chinese strategist Sun-tzu. Beijing’s longtime “one country, two systems” policy toward Taiwan—which would bring the island under Chinese sovereignty while allowing it substantial autonomy—leaves room for a negotiated solution. Last year, in an important analysis of China’s strategic ambitions, the scholars David Kang, Jackie Wong, and Zenobia Chan examined 12,000 articles and hundreds of speeches by Xi to argue that Beijing prioritizes regime stability and internal development over displacing the United States as a global military hegemon. Although China’s major, unannounced exercises around Taiwan earlier this month may constitute an exception, overall, Chinese military exercises near Taiwan over the last five years are consistent with this interpretation: many have reacted to perceived provocations, illustrate only medium-scale capabilities, and have generally had a performative quality, accompanied by extensive press coverage.

There is still an opening for the United States and China to de-escalate. To achieve strategic stability, the two countries will need to make a concerted effort to defuse their security dilemma—the dynamic in which each side perceives the other’s defensive measures as aggressive. First, U.S. and Chinese leaders need to revive superpower summitry. Biden held only three face-to-face meetings with Xi on the sidelines of regional economic meetings. Although the May 2026 Beijing summit between Trump and Xi yielded no major breakthroughs, the meeting did improve the two leaders’ personal relationship; today, U.S.-Chinese tensions are lower than they were between 2021 and 2025. This progress suggests the great value of holding a regular, annual, multiday bilateral summit with a significant focus on nuclear arms control. Such meetings would give expert working groups time to methodically develop measures to reduce the risk of conflict. Just as important, the summits need to be long enough to leave room for unscripted conversations. At their first summit, in Geneva in November 1985, U.S. President Ronald Reagan and Soviet leader Mikhail Gorbachev famously broke away from anxious bureaucrats and advisers for an impromptu walk—an encounter that helped establish a more constructive relationship.

The United States must also take concrete steps to reduce the chance of escalation in the Taiwan Strait. Above all, it should adhere more consistently to its “one China” policy—its position that Beijing is the sole legal government of China. More consistency would include limiting U.S. legislators’ trips to the island and downgrading U.S.-Taiwanese defense relations by, for example, holding meetings less frequently and at lower diplomatic levels. Washington should remind key allies such as Japan to proceed cautiously in terms of their rhetoric and military deployments. Such a restraint-oriented strategy would make it clear to Taiwan that independence is off the table, encouraging it to take a more accommodating approach that would lower the temperature with Beijing. If Washington were to agree to common-sense limitations on arms sales to Taiwan—such as refusing to sell Taiwan the surface-to-surface ballistic Army Tactical Missile System (ATACMS), which can reach across the strait to the Chinese mainland—Beijing would likely reciprocate by scaling back its military exercises and related military deployments around the island. There are real incentives for Beijing to de-escalate. Doing so could improve relations with neighbors such as Japan, free up focus for domestic priorities, lower military spending, and limit the chance of war.

The Pentagon needs to demonstrate Washington’s intent to avoid war over Taiwan by gradually decreasing U.S. warship transits through the Taiwan Strait. Even more important, Washington should also substantially reduce U.S. military deployments in the Luzon Strait between Taiwan and the Philippines and around Japan’s Ryukyu Islands. Such deployments only exacerbate the security dilemma.

Exhibiting restraint does not mean the United States would be giving Taiwan away. The United States can continue to provide Taiwan with defensive arms (such as missile interceptors, artillery, drones, and mines) and harden U.S. bases in the region to provide stabilizing deterrence. But even if U.S. leaders publicly abjure direct military intervention to defend Taiwan—a rational step that would lower the risk of nuclear war—China will prefer negotiating with Taipei on the island’s autonomy over a bloody military showdown with Taiwanese forces. For their part, Taiwan’s leaders will likely accommodate themselves to limited self-governance under Chinese overall sovereignty. In the end, China’s main goal is to get Taiwan out of the U.S. sphere of influence. Chinese leaders’ major fear is not so much Taiwanese autonomy as the prospect of the island serving as a U.S. forward base—in Chinese parlance, “using Taiwan to contain China.”

Another important step would be to seek a multilateral pledge by all nuclear-armed states to employ nuclear weapons only in retaliation against a nuclear attack. Such a policy would reduce the risk of miscalculation during a crisis. China has long embraced such a no-first-use policy, but U.S. leaders have traditionally opposed it, believing that it might expose U.S. allies to conventional attack. The U.S. nuclear deterrent for allies, however, increasingly lacks credibility because the costs of starting a nuclear war would vastly outweigh the benefits. And after the collapse of traditional nuclear arms control, a mutual no-first-use policy offers a promising area of common ground between the United States and China. It would reinforce the existing no-first-use policies of China and India and implicitly acknowledge their leadership in the area of nuclear restraint, a virtue when newer nuclear states are chafing under the old Cold War superpowers’ dominance of arms control. This approach could even entice Beijing into a wider, more comprehensive process of bilateral or multilateral nuclear arms control.

Beyond these three major initiatives, the United States and China should sign an agreement to notify each other about their ballistic missile tests. Washington could also consider giving Beijing a larger role in nonproliferation efforts, for instance by affording China leadership roles in negotiations and inspection regimes in future nonproliferation arrangements. The U.S. arms control agenda with China should focus not only on offensive nuclear systems but also on missile defenses and tactical nuclear weapons, as well as AI technologies. And it would help if the United States evolved its own nuclear posture in the direction of restraint by reducing its warheads, warfighting capabilities, and alert posture.

Ultimately, the United States needs to get serious about reining in an arms race with China before its perception that one is apace becomes a self-fulfilling prophecy. The two countries have a mutual interest in restraint. If Washington continues to overlook Beijing’s incentives to compromise—and the real signs that it is willing to do so—an arms race that, as of now, is mostly putative will become a reality. And a catastrophic war that could have been avoided could indeed become inevitable.

#5835. Democrats Want to Make Life More Affordable. They Could Begin With Permitting Reform.

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Democrats Want to Make Life More Affordable. They Could Begin With Permitting Reform.

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The Democratic wave that shook the U.S. political landscape this month delivered a striking rebuke to President Donald Trump and put affordability squarely at the top of the political agenda. A bill that passed the Senate last week to reform the nation’s permitting laws offers Democrats—who voted two-to-one against the bill in the Senate—an early test of whether they can make daily life more affordable for Americans, even before taking control in January. Permitting reform may seem like a narrow or technical topic, but this is the most important piece of energy legislation in years. By speeding up environmental reviews, limiting litigation delays, and easing transmission siting, it would expand clean energy, lower energy prices, strengthen our energy system, and make future climate action more effective.

The bill, known as the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, or BAAJA, would make it easier to build transmission lines and other energy infrastructure, including both clean energy and oil and gas projects. That breadth has delighted advocates of abundance, the movement that argues that shortcomings in public infrastructure stem from regulatory bottlenecks, and enraged many environmentalists. Some supporters have been too quick to dismiss legitimate concerns, while some climate activists have drowned out substantive but highly technical legal objections with reflexive anti-fossil fuel rhetoric. As the bill heads to a House vote, a careful assessment of its provisions should lead members to support it while pursuing opportunities where possible to address credible objections in the final stages of the legislative process.

The Democratic wave that shook the U.S. political landscape this month delivered a striking rebuke to President Donald Trump and put affordability squarely at the top of the political agenda. A bill that passed the Senate last week to reform the nation’s permitting laws offers Democrats—who voted two-to-one against the bill in the Senate—an early test of whether they can make daily life more affordable for Americans, even before taking control in January. Permitting reform may seem like a narrow or technical topic, but this is the most important piece of energy legislation in years. By speeding up environmental reviews, limiting litigation delays, and easing transmission siting, it would expand clean energy, lower energy prices, strengthen our energy system, and make future climate action more effective.

The bill, known as the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, or BAAJA, would make it easier to build transmission lines and other energy infrastructure, including both clean energy and oil and gas projects. That breadth has delighted advocates of abundance, the movement that argues that shortcomings in public infrastructure stem from regulatory bottlenecks, and enraged many environmentalists. Some supporters have been too quick to dismiss legitimate concerns, while some climate activists have drowned out substantive but highly technical legal objections with reflexive anti-fossil fuel rhetoric. As the bill heads to a House vote, a careful assessment of its provisions should lead members to support it while pursuing opportunities where possible to address credible objections in the final stages of the legislative process.

Objections to the permitting reform bill fall into several broad categories. First, some Democrats and environmental groups argue that the blue election wave gives them leverage to secure a better deal next year, yet that misreads both the politics and the policy. A larger Democratic majority does not necessarily mean a majority for blocking fossil fuel infrastructure. The incoming caucus includes members who support oil and gas development alongside clean energy—in states such as Texas, Mississippi, Kansas, South Carolina, and Alaska. Nor does 56 Senate seats eliminate the need for Republican votes to overcome a filibuster. And Trump will still be president, with the power to veto the bill.

Second, some environmental groups oppose the bill because it weakens tools of environmental review and permitting often used to block fossil fuel projects such as oil and gas pipelines. Yet ample evidence demonstrates that trying to “keep it in the ground”—stymying fossil fuel supply by blocking infrastructure—does little to reduce global oil and gas use, which ultimately is what matters for greenhouse gas emissions, and can trigger backlash to climate action if energy security and affordability are perceived to suffer as a result.

In a global market, supply restrictions in one location merely lead to increases elsewhere unless demand falls. Higher prices can curb demand, but this year’s energy crisis offers further evidence that political and economic pressures push elected leaders to use every available tool to bring prices back down. The Iran war led to the closure of much of the Strait of Hormuz, through which roughly 20 percent of the world’s oil flowed, yet oil demand this year will drop only 2.5 percent, according to the International Energy Agency (IEA).

While it is possible—and necessary—to reduce the need for oil and gas over the long term, today fossil fuels still supply roughly four-fifths of the world’s energy, a figure that has barely changed in decades. While reducing that dependence is urgently needed to address the rapidly escalating threats of climate change, the hard reality is that keeping energy affordable and reliable during a multidecade transition still requires investment in oil and gas. According to the IEA, around $490 billion a year would still be needed by 2035 even in a scenario where countries meet their climate pledges—which the collapse of United Nations climate talks in Turkey made plainly clear is far from being true today.

Moreover, the same strategies used against high-profile pipelines such as Keystone XL and Mountain Valley have often proved effective at obstructing clean energy projects as well. As the New York Times editorial board argued this month in endorsing permitting reform, essential laws protecting the environment, communities, and Indigenous peoples “have become so complex and restrictive that the country cannot build the future it needs.”

To be sure, the BAAJA is not a substitute for climate policy, nor are its emissions benefits assured. Analysis by Rhodium Group, a consultancy, projects that the bill could reduce U.S. power-sector emissions in 2040 by as much as 18 percent compared with current policy, or by as little as 1 percent, depending on energy prices and technology costs. At the same time, climate policy cannot be effective without a bill such as the BAAJA that allows for the rapid buildout of the generation, transmission, storage, and transit infrastructure required for cleaner energy solutions.

A third area of concern involves weakened tribal consultation under the National Historic Preservation Act. Faster permitting should not undermine tribes’ ability to meaningfully influence decisions that affect sacred sites and other places central to their history and traditions. A set of revisions to the bill would have expanded the projects subject to tribal consultation and authorized additional money to support tribal and state historic-preservation work, yet Democratic Senator Jeff Merkley prevented those amendments from being considered, which may make it harder for some House Democrats to support the bill.

Expanding the U.S. grid is vital to staying globally competitive.

Ones and Tooze on permitting reform and what that means for U.S. energy production.

Finally, some opponents worry that specific provisions could protect improperly approved projects, require the government to pay companies large sums in damages, and constrain future administrations’ ability to issue or enforce environmental protections. Because these objections involve detailed, often technical, legal objections to the bill’s drafting, they have failed to garner the same attention as absolutist rhetoric about fossil fuels or misleading claims about data center buildout, which is unfortunate because many of these legal concerns have merit, even if they do not ultimately justify opposing the bill.

At the heart of critics’ concerns is a mistrust of how a conservative judiciary hostile to environmental regulation will interpret the bill’s ambiguities. Key areas of concern include new limits on National Environmental Policy Act lawsuits and potential unintended consequences of provisions to limit an administration’s ability to revoke existing permits. Those so-called permit certainty provisions, which the New Yorker’s Elizabeth Kolbert called the most “dangerous” in the bill, were designed to prevent just the sort of harm the Trump administration has done to offshore wind.

Yet subsequent revisions to the bill, known as the “manager’s amendment,” made meaningful improvements to address those concerns. Kolbert writes that the bill could “tie the hands of any future Administration that wants to responsibly promote green energy over fossil fuels,” yet the revisions specifically added an exception to address this concern for agency actions taken under applicable law or regulation and a clause intended to preserve existing legal authorities. They responded to concerns that restrictions on government interference with a permitted project’s operation at “full capacity” could be used to block new environmental rules—for example, emissions standards under the Clean Air Act that would induce a coal plant to curb its output. The amendment also extends from five to 10 years the permitting-data lookback used to assess claims of disparate treatment. That longer time period addressed concerns that Trump’s preferential treatment of fossil fuels and obstruction of renewables would become the benchmark against which a future administration’s permitting decisions are judged.

The changes also require a showing of bad faith for the additional damages available under the bill, addressing concerns that an agency could face enormous payouts for an action a court finds unlawful. And the amendment expressly allows the government to offer lawful explanations for differences in permitting outcomes and extends the requirement that applicants seeking relief demonstrate technical and financial capacity to all of their pending projects.

But the changes do not eliminate every risk. For example, an expansive interpretation of what counts as a “permit” or “authorization” could, critics fear, extend protections beyond formal agency approvals to informal assurances from the Trump administration. Critics also worry that because nearly every agency action invokes some legal authority, a broad reading of the exception for actions “taken pursuant to any applicable law or regulation” could render the permit protections meaningless. To avoid that result, judges could read the exception narrowly, limiting future administrations’ ability to curb pollution.

Another round of revisions—the one blocked by Merkley—would have made modest improvements, such as to the concern about private assurances counting as authorizations. The legal risks identified about how hostile judges could interpret ambiguity merit attention and should be remedied to the extent possible as the current bill moves through Congress. Although the bill is unlikely to be amended in the House, one of its architects in the Senate has suggested the House might attach amendments to other pieces of must-pass legislation.

Even without those revisions, however, the bill’s benefits outweigh the risks. The current permitting system simply makes it too difficult and costly to expand the grid, build a stronger energy system, connect cleaner generation, and lower energy costs.

Later this week, Americans will gather for Thanksgiving, with energy costs weighing on the price of traveling to see family, heating homes, and cooking the holiday meal. The annual national observance of Thanksgiving started with President Abraham Lincoln’s proclamation in 1863—the same year construction began on the transcontinental railroad. Six years later, the two lines met in Utah, connecting nearly 1,800 miles of track. By contrast, California today aims to begin high-speed rail service between Merced and Bakersfield—a distance of less than 200 miles—in 2033, a quarter-century after voters approved the project. When members of Congress return from their Thanksgiving celebrations, their first order of business should be to make it easier to build again in this country—starting with the infrastructure needed for a cleaner, more affordable, and more reliable energy system.

Jason Bordoff is a columnist at Foreign Policy and the founding director of Columbia University’s Center on Global Energy Policy. He is a professor of professional practice in international and public affairs, co-founding dean emeritus at the Columbia Climate School, and former senior director on the staff of the U.S. National Security Council and special assistant to former U.S. President Barack Obama. #5834. GOP senator 'not likely' to mount presidential bid, but continues leaving door open to possibility

While Sen. John Kennedy says that he plans to seek re-election, he is leaving the door open to potentially run for president in 2028. (NBC News)

Sen. John Kennedy, R-La., is continuing to leave the door open to the possibility of mounting a 2028 presidential bid, though he said that such a move is "not likely" and that he plans to seek re-election.

"My plan right now is to run for re-election," Kennedy told Kristen Welker during an interview that aired on NBC's "Meet the Press" on Sunday.

"If I get fed up with things and catch a wild hair and have a, I don't know, a spontaneous moment of ... questionable genius, if I get really fed up I could run. But it's not likely," he said, noting that he plans to pursue re-election.

Sen. John Kennedy, R-La., during a Senate Judiciary Committee hearing in Washington, D.C., on Tuesday, Sept. 29, 2026. (Aaron Schwartz/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

#5833. Why Israel Is Losing America

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREW P. MILLER is a Senior Fellow in National Security and International Policy at the Center for American Progress. He was Deputy Assistant Secretary of State for Israeli-Palestinian Affairs in the Biden administration and Director for Egypt and Israel Military Issues on the National Security Council in the Obama administration.

Only a Radical Course Change Can Win Back Its Most Important Ally

American politicians have woken up to the fact that the public has soured on Israel. On November 3, as Americans headed to the polls, U.S. President Donald Trump made the surprising announcement that the United States would stop sending aid to Israel, valued at around $4 billion annually, by the end of the year. That night, Democrats, many of whom are increasingly outspoken in their criticism of Israeli actions, swept the House and Senate.

As I wrote in Foreign Affairs a year ago, the “exceptional relationship” between the United States and Israel, in which Israel is treated differently than any other U.S. ally or partner, is coming to an end. No other country receives the tremendous latitude and unconditional backing that the United States extends to Israel. In addition to directly hurting Palestinians, the United States’ support for Israel incentivizes reckless Israeli behavior and dubious strategic thinking. The consequences of the blank check are clear. Over the past three years, tens of thousands of Gazans have been killed, progress toward peace and reconstruction in the Gaza Strip has stalled, the annexation of the West Bank proceeds at an unprecedented rate, and the United States is stuck in an unpopular war in the Middle East with seemingly no end. Israel, for its part, is no safer than on October 6, 2023, and unquestionably more isolated.

Things are about to change. For better or worse, the United States will recalibrate its relationship with Israel over the coming years and a new paradigm for the bilateral relationship will emerge—one in which Israel is expected to comply with U.S. and international law, the two countries refrain from interfering in each other’s domestic politics, and the United States limits its support when Israeli goals conflict with its own.

This change is inevitable, and there is little that Israel and its supporters can do to stop it. But Israel can help keep the United States onside by making itself a reliable partner. As it begins what could be a drawn-out government formation process after last month’s elections, Israel can opt to change direction and plot a course that will not further alienate its closest ally.

Whoever leads the next Israeli government can begin rebuilding trust with the United States before a new U.S. president takes office, in 2029. It won’t be easy. Just as Americans have grown critical of Israel over the past three years, Israelis have shifted further to the right. Israel’s next leaders will need the courage to curb the country’s expansionist policies and resurrect the much-maligned two-state solution. But if they fail to overhaul their approach to Palestinians and the wider region, Israel risks losing its biggest champion and defender—perhaps for good.

Trump, who seems to delight in bullying U.S. allies and partners, has maintained the United States’ long-standing exceptional approach to Israel. He joined Israel in launching strikes on Iran in June 2025 and a larger war in February 2026, embarking for the first time on a truly joint military operation with the Jewish state. Like other presidents before him, Trump has provided Israel with bombs, missiles, and aircraft, largely with no strings attached. He and Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu have also interfered in each other’s political systems to an unprecedented degree by endorsing candidates.

Time and again, Trump has deferred to Israel even when such support has run against American interests. The 2026 war on Iran is the most blatant example. Netanyahu persuaded Trump to give up nuclear talks with Iran and join a war that has failed in its objectives and upended energy markets. Even where the Trump administration has staked out U.S. priorities, it has largely refrained from trying to compel Israel to adhere to them. Israel, for example, has obstructed Trump’s peace plan for Gaza, which is meant to restore governance to the Palestinians, disarm Hamas, and lead to a permanent Israeli withdrawal from the territory. Israel has undermined the plan by expanding its military footprint in Gaza beyond the agreed demarcation lines and denying entry to the National Committee for the Administration of Gaza (NCAG), an interim technocratic body that is intended to govern the territory under Trump’s plan and is currently marooned in Egypt. But the Trump administration has barely sounded a single note of disapproval, reinforcing Netanyahu’s long-standing view that he can defy the United States at little cost.

Moreover, it remains to be seen whether Trump will follow through on his pledge to cut military aid to Israel. The timing of his statement suggests it could have been an electoral stunt, and he said himself that the announcement would deprive the left of a talking point. One possibility is that the United States continues gifting Israel weapons to the tune of billions, just under a different guise. The National Defense Authorization Act for fiscal year 2027, which passed the House this summer and could pass the Senate before Democrats take control of both chambers of Congress this coming January, contains a provision that would expand the Pentagon’s financial and material assistance to Israel.

Trump has made no effort to bring U.S. military assistance or transfers to Israel in line with what it provides to other countries. For decades, the United States has been reluctant to subject arms sales to Israel to U.S. regulations or condition them on Israeli behavior. All recent presidents, for example, have effectively exempted Israel from so-called Leahy laws, which prohibit U.S. assistance to foreign military units credibly implicated in human-rights violations. Despite rampant settler terrorism in the West Bank, in many cases facilitated by the Israeli military, the Trump administration has not indicated that it would declare any Israeli units ineligible to receive U.S. weapons.

Trump has also intervened in Israeli politics more than any other U.S. leader. He has hectored Israeli President Isaac Herzog to pardon Netanyahu so that the prime minister can escape the consequences of an ongoing corruption trial. Ahead of October’s election in Israel, Trump offered Netanyahu a “complete and total endorsement” in his bid for another term. Such blatant interference in Israel’s domestic politics not only undermines the rule of law and integrity of Israeli elections but also serves to normalize Netanyahu’s repeated interventions in U.S. domestic politics. True to form, the Israeli prime minister has praised Trump as “by far the greatest friend Israel has ever had or will have in the White House” just two weeks before the U.S. midterm elections, effectively endorsing Republican candidates for Congress. As in other areas of the relationship, Trump has made what is already an unhealthy dynamic worse.

Unconditional support from the United States has hurt Israel in a crucial way: it indulges the worst instincts of Israeli leaders and does nothing to dissuade them from their embrace of a near-perpetual state of war. After Hamas’s attack three years ago, Israel has bombed Gaza, Iran, Lebanon, Syria, Yemen, and even Qatar. Although Israel has dealt blows to its adversaries, there is no evidence that these wars have made Israel any safer. Iran and its proxies have been weakened, but in the course of fighting, Tehran has discovered the extent of its leverage over the Strait of Hormuz and the global economy and appears even more interested in developing a nuclear weapon. In Lebanon, Hezbollah still poses a threat and is devising new ways of striking Israeli territory. Although confined to a smaller portion of Gaza, Hamas is reconstituting its capabilities and quashing any internal Palestinian challenge to its rule. And in the West Bank, runaway settler violence, the proliferation of illegal Israeli outposts, and the growing threat of terrorism or even a Palestinian uprising have pushed the territory to the breaking point.

Israel has also drifted ever closer to pariah status, isolated internationally thanks to its aggressive tactics. Even the country’s closest historical allies have imposed punitive measures that would have been unthinkable just five years ago. Canada, France, and the United Kingdom, for example, announced wide-ranging sanctions against Israeli settlements in the West Bank in September. Such policies are no longer only the purview of traditional critics of Israel, such as Indonesia and Ireland.

Israel’s reputation within the United States has also tanked. According to Pew polling, 60 percent of Americans have an unfavorable view of Israel, up from 42 percent in 2022. So far, Trump has stopped Congress from restricting support for Israel—both through threatening vetoes and applying pressure on congressional Republicans—but he cannot hold back the tide of American public opinion indefinitely. Once it breaks, Israelis will have to face more than criticism from its closest ally and security guarantor. With Democrats soon to be in control of both the House and the Senate, Congress will likely pass bills that sanction settlement building and condition military aid on adherence to U.S. and international law.

Without changes in U.S. and Israeli policy, the two countries could see their relationship rupture as continued support for Israel becomes politically untenable in the United States. To rehabilitate itself in the eyes of Americans—and therefore ensure long-term cooperation with the United States—Israel will need to change course. It must abandon Netanyahu’s bludgeoning approach to both the Palestinians and the wider region.

It is no exaggeration to say that the future of the U.S.-Israeli relationship rests on what type of government Israel forms in the next few months. Netanyahu’s exclusion from the next government represents the best hope for preserving bilateral relations with the United States. Netanyahu has proved unwilling to stay out of U.S. politics or modify draconian security policies that have run afoul of the American public. Unfortunately, most of Netanyahu’s rivals for prime minister share his hawkish position on the Palestinians and the Middle East. Were they in power, they seem likely to resist fundamental revisions to Israeli policy that could safeguard the bilateral relationship.

Israel’s next leaders need to journey outside their comfort zone. They will have to come to terms with new realities: that tensions between the United States and Israel are now deep-rooted and that they can’t rely on the adamantine support of the Republican party any longer. To win back Americans, the Israeli government will have to rigorously follow through on its commitments under Trump’s plan for Gaza, daring Hamas to follow suit. In addition to flooding Gaza with humanitarian aid, Israel should let the NCAG enter the territory to offer an alternative to Hamas’s rule and nihilistic agenda. Enabling the deployment of newly trained Palestinian security forces and the formation of the International Stabilization Force, a peacekeeping mission, would also help show that, whatever unfolds in Gaza, Israel is operating in good faith. Israeli forces should immediately return to the demarcation lines traced in Trump’s Gaza peace plan and pull back even further in concert with Hamas’s progress in fulfilling the terms of the plan until Israel has completely withdrawn from Gaza.

Israel must also rein in violent settlers in the West Bank and hold them accountable through the courts. The Israeli security services can identify the leaders and primary instigators behind the violence and should arrest them and bring them to justice. Given that settlers have killed and injured American citizens in these attacks, continued inaction by Israeli authorities is a recipe for more friction with the United States, especially if a Democratic government comes to power in Washington.

Israel must also halt its creeping annexation of the West Bank. Over the past three years, the Netanyahu government has increased its military presence in the West Bank, deprived the Palestinian Authority of funds, asserted Israeli government control over larger swaths of Palestinian land, approved a slew of new Israeli settlements, and retroactively legalized illicit outposts. In sum, these actions aim to render a Palestinian state impossible. Although reversing these measures would not arrest the expansion of existing settlements and Israeli infrastructure throughout the West Bank, they would be received positively in the United States.

Israeli leaders also need to break the taboo around Palestinian statehood. Only a couple of decades ago, the idea of a Palestinian state was conceivable to most Israelis. Today, Israeli politicians, with precious few exceptions, cannot bring themselves to endorse a two-state solution. It will take time and no small degree of political resolve, but Israeli leaders must convince the public to accept the necessity and the virtues of a Palestinian state existing side by side with Israel. An Israel that can view Palestinians as neighbors entitled to the same rights would provide a very different image to the world than a messianic government bent on expelling whole populations.

These steps will not satisfy all American critics of Israel. Anti-Israeli attitudes cannot be erased in a matter of months. Where anti-Israeli opinion intersects with anti-Semitism—evident in incidents such as a recent boycott of Jewish-owned restaurants in Philadelphia organized by the group Philly Bike on Your Left—the task is even more difficult and will necessitate a different set of policies that transcend Israel, including the formation of diverse domestic and international coalitions to identify and condemn anti-Semitic acts. Yet a far less belligerent Israel and one newly committed to the resolution of the unanswered question of Palestinian self-determination would start to shift U.S. public opinion back in the country’s favor.

If Israel fails to change course, it will find itself less secure and more isolated internationally. Without access to international markets and capital, its economy, which has withstood the turmoil of the last several years remarkably well, will suffer and living standards will fall. Under these circumstances, Jewish Israeli citizens with second passports may elect to leave, depriving the state of its raison d’être—providing a safe and prosperous homeland for the Jewish people. Put simply, an Israel without American support will struggle to survive.

A Failing Peace Process Leads to Only One Outcome

The Netanyahu Doctrine Could Outlast Netanyahu

How Syria Can Capitalize on the Post-Hormuz Economy

Arab Citizens of Israel Can Bring Change, but They Can’t Do It Alone

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

#5832. Jensen Huang Is Back

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: Jensen Huang Is Back

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the return of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang, collapsed negotiations at this year’s U.N. climate summit, and Ukraine’s naval drone attack.

Nvidia, the semiconductor giant at the center of the global artificial intelligence race, announced on Monday that it had reinstated its CEO, Jensen Huang, just 11 days after he was forced to resign following multiple alleged extramarital affairs.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at the return of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang, collapsed negotiations at this year’s U.N. climate summit, and Ukraine’s naval drone attack.

Nvidia, the semiconductor giant at the center of the global artificial intelligence race, announced on Monday that it had reinstated its CEO, Jensen Huang, just 11 days after he was forced to resign following multiple alleged extramarital affairs.

Monday’s announcement marks a dramatic development in a saga that has roiled markets, seen the powerful company’s value plunge, and fueled uncertainty about the future of the AI boom. Plummeting stock prices had wiped some $2.6 trillion from Nvidia’s market value, and amid mounting scrutiny, the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission reportedly launched a probe into the company’s accounting practices.

Huang’s alleged affairs were made particularly scandalous—and geopolitically noteworthy—by the fact that one of those affairs was with a woman whom U.S. intelligence agencies suspect of being a Chinese spy. That revelation raised major national security concerns for a company whose most advanced AI chips remained banned from China.

Yet all those concerns now appear to have been cast aside at the behest of Huang’s most powerful backer: U.S. President Donald Trump. Trump has never wavered from Huang’s corner, calling him a “very high-quality person” and an “amazing guy” even after the Nvidia CEO resigned. Now, it appears that the U.S. president decided to put his thumb on the scale to bring Huang back.

On Monday, Nvidia’s stock price rose by 33 percent in the wake of the company’s announcement.

The Nvidia board said it had “received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the Board is confident that Mr. Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.”

Huang said in a statement shared by the board that he was “honored” to return to Nvidia and would do so with “renewed commitment.” The board, meanwhile, said its previously announced independent investigation into Huang’s possible Chinese espionage exposure would proceed as planned and that Huang had pledged to cooperate with it.

Trump, who posted on Truth Social Sunday night that he hoped the Nvidia board would reinstate Huang, also hinted on Monday that such a decision was coming, even before it was officially confirmed.

“He’s back where he should be,” Trump said in a phone interview with PBS NewsHour minutes before the Nvidia announcement. “Well, he will be soon, hopefully,” he added. Trump also told CNBC after the announcement that he had spoken directly to Nvidia’s board and provided them with assurances on the national security threat. “I convinced them,” he said. “I made the deal.” Afterward, Trump called Huang and asked him to “work hard,” he told CNBC.

Some U.S. lawmakers from Trump’s own party appear to be less convinced. “Jensen Huang was compromised and exploited by a Chinese spy. Nvidia should have moved on without Huang and ignored the Administration’s inexplicable desire to save Huang,” Republican Rep. Don Bacon wrote in a post on X.

Texas Sen. John Cornyn was more pointed in his own X post. “No national security risk? Based on what?” Cornyn wrote. (Both Cornyn and Bacon are leaving Congress at the end of this year.)

Utah Sen. John Curtis also slammed the decision, declaring in a post on X that Huang “was right to step down when the affair came to light.” Curtis also wrote, “I have some questions. Who in our government weighed in? What did they tell the board? And on what basis did the board conclude there was no risk?”

Trump dismissed those concerns in his CNBC interview, agreeing when CNBC asked whether Republicans would “come around” on Huang’s reinstatement. As for the national security concerns, Trump dismissed new reporting that the Chinese woman whom Huang had a romantic relationship with, Alice Huang, was in contact with China’s Ministry of State Security—the country’s main intelligence agency—during their affair. “Who isn’t in contact?” Trump asked. “China speaks to everybody,” he said, adding, “They speak to me.”

Trump said he had spoken to Chinese President Xi Jinping “at length” about Jensen Huang during the two leaders’ meeting in Shenzhen, China, last week. The U.S. president also claimed that keeping Huang out of Nvidia would risk the CEO defecting to China. “I could see China coming in and saying to Jensen: ‘Hey, how would you like to come to China?’” Trump said. “He’s got a little blood in him, you know,” the U.S. president added, alluding to Huang’s Taiwanese heritage.

After the interview, Trump made his backing of Huang far more explicit with another post on Truth Social. The post featured an AI-generated recasting of a photo of Trump raising his fist after an assassination attempt in 2024; instead of Trump, the AI-generated version depicted Huang with an Nvidia flag in the background, raising his fist with a red bra in hand. “Sex sells,” Trump told CNBC.

Wednesday, Nov. 25: An EU-Western Balkans summit kicks off in Brussels.

Saturday, Nov. 28: Taiwan holds local elections.

Sunday, Nov. 29: Spain holds snap parliamentary elections.

Deep divisions. Negotiations at this year’s U.N. climate summit, also known as COP31, collapsed on Sunday as countries clashed over a proposed deal and ultimately failed to reach consensus. It marks the first time that the climate talks have failed over a final deal in about 17 years, and it comes in a year that has seen record-shattering heat and other climate extremes.

The proposed deal had omitted any mention of fossil fuels, the primary driver of climate change. That exclusion sparked an uproar and prompted Pacific island nations and the European Union, among others, to reject it.

The proposed agreement “failed to meet the moment,” said Irish Environment Minister Darragh O’Brien, who was speaking on behalf of the European Union. “We will not endorse a decision that does not respond to the science and that actually risks deepening the chasm between global commitments and the action required.”

Maritime strikes. Ukrainian forces deployed seven naval drones in a weekend attack against two Russian vessels located off the coast of Libya, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky said in a post on X on Monday. The strikes sank the Russian corvette Merkury, which is one of Moscow’s newest ships, Zelensky said.

Drones also struck a Russian-owned cargo ship in the Baltic Sea on Saturday; the vessel later floated into Finnish waters. Finnish authorities believe that Ukraine perpetrated the attack, but Kyiv has not taken responsibility for it. “War is spilling over into our immediate vicinity,” Finnish Prime Minister Petteri Orpo said in a post on X, though he added that “Ukraine has the right to fight back against the aggressor.”

Under pressure. South Korea’s education minister resigned on Sunday amid controversy over newly announced anti-cheating measures. After news emerged of a cheating scandal in a separate exam, South Korean officials unveiled a raft of rigid anti-AI cheating measures just days before the country’s annual college entrance exam, known as the Suneung.

The Suneung is a famously grueling eight-hour test that can be vital in shaping students’ career outcomes. Yet the last-minute announcement of the new rules, which included blaring emergency cellphone alerts, sparked confusion among test-takers. Ultimately, some 111 people were caught cheating, according to officials.

“I believe I am not free from criticism or blame from the public for failing to prepare adequately in advance,” the education minister said in a statement reported by local media.

On a stage in Ottawa over the weekend, Trump joked around with two Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carneys—or did he? The Canadian leader actually recruited two comedians to assist him with a roast at an annual dinner with journalists, enlisting impersonators both of himself and his U.S. counterpart. “I am pressing the emergency comedy button,” Carney joked.

#5831. 'Failed socialist state': Deep blue state’s lone GOP congressman reveals secret to winning as Democrats sweep

Rep. Cliff Bentz, R-Oregon, told Fox News Digital that the high cost of living in his state, coupled by what he claims are failed socialist policies ravishing through his communities, drove voters to back him one more in his re-election efforts.

EXCLUSIVE: Oregon's lone Republican congressman told Fox News Digital that voters in his district rejected what he described as the state's "failed" progressive policies, offering the GOP a roadmap for winning back voters after Democrats' midterm sweep.

Rep. Cliff Bentz, R-Ore., who won his fourth term in Congress by over 70,000 votes, with a total of 61% of the vote, made his remarks after securing re-election despite a nationwide Democratic wave that handed Republicans significant losses in Congress and state elections.

"My folks wanted someone that they could depend upon when it comes to protecting business and trying to reverse this horrible trend toward more, in this you have a failed state than anything else," Bentz told Fox News Digital.

FLORIDA CONGRESSMAN DIAGNOSES GOP'S BLUE TSUNAMI LOSSES: 'WE FAILED TO MAKE THEM THINK WE CARED ABOUT THEM'

Rep. Cliff Bentz suggested that "When you're in the minority, you can start calling out the stupidity of the opponent's approach to the economy and to regulation." (Cliff Bentz)

The congressman argued that his continued electoral success stems from highlighting what he considers the economic consequences of Democratic leadership in Oregon, particularly policies enacted in the state capital.

"Crazy, crazy Democrat policies emanating out of Salem, the capital of Oregon," Bentz said, pointing to what he described as "the ridiculous impact of these policies."

Bentz blamed high taxes and progressive regulations for hurting businesses and contributing to Oregon's declining quality of life, arguing that voters are increasingly frustrated with the state's political direction.

Oregon has a 9.9% state income tax rate, a top corporate income tax of 7.6% and a 0.57% corporate activity tax rate, which he believes has driven the local economy to a near collapse. The state legislature has recently enacted policies such as ones that require certain large employers in retail, hospitality and food service to provide advance work schedules, which critics argue limits scheduling flexibility.

REPUBLICANS NEED HISTORY LESSON ABOUT THEIR PARTY IF THEY WANT TO MOVE ON FROM DEFEAT

The Republican also suggested that his party could regain ground nationally by adopting a similar approach, particularly as Republicans prepare to enter the minority in Congress.

Democrats outnumber Republicans by nearly 100 seats in the House, and 10 seats in the Senate.

Ronald Ross, Multnomah County outreach supervisor with the 4D Recovery Center, speaks to a person in a tent about services for those experiencing homelessness and efforts to offer mental health support and treatment for fentanyl addiction in Portland, Oregon on January 24, 2024. (Photo by Patrick T. Fallon/AFP/Getty Images)

"When you're in the minority, you can start calling out the stupidity of the opponent's approach to the economy and to regulation," Bentz told Fox News Digital.

"So you start out by saying, 'Hey, if you want more of this, well, just keep voting for it,'" he continued.

Bentz argued that Republicans should use their time in the minority to highlight the economic consequences of Democratic policies and present themselves as an alternative to progressive governance.

Voters cast their ballots at official ballot boxes on November 8, 2022, in Portland, Oregon. (Photo by Mathieu Lewis-Rolland/Getty Images)

"I think that's hugely important, and that's what I'll be doing in these next two years," Bentz added.

#5830. The Fantasy of a Turkish Middle East

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MELIHA BENLI ALTUNISIK is Professor of International Relations at Middle East Technical University.

Ankara’s Risky Bid to Impose Regional Order

Since Hamas’s October 7, 2023, attack on Israel, the future of the Middle East has been fiercely contested. The previous Gulf-centered order had Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates (UAE) trying to stabilize and shape the region using economic statecraft and the normalization of relations with former foes in Iran, Israel, Turkey, and Syria. Three years of war in Gaza, and more recently in Iran and the Gulf, have severely disrupted that order-building project and generated a deep sense of uncertainty about what comes next.

Turkey, like every major power in the Middle East, has tried to fill the vacuum. Before 2023, Ankara’s influence had been in decline; after years of diplomatic isolation following the Arab uprisings, it had just begun repairing its ties with major regional actors. But in the past few years, Turkey has earned American backing for its policies in Syria and its regional diplomacy, and it has made an effort to secure the home front by pursuing a peace process with the Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK), a militant group that has fought a decades-long insurgency against the Turkish state. Most recently, in August, Pakistan, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey signed the Mecca Defense Alliance, a collective security agreement that institutionalizes Turkey’s regional positioning and pulls the country directly into Saudi Arabia’s war with the Houthis.

Turkey’s post–October 7 strategy has restored the country’s position as a regional heavyweight. But it is not capable of meeting its larger objective: establishing order on its own terms. Turkey, like the region’s other aspiring leaders, lacks the power to impose a new security framework on its own. Its nascent efforts to build one, such as the new Mecca pact, merely make countries outside the bloc feel unsafe, feeding instability. Turkey’s most ambitious plays for regional leadership are therefore likely to prove ineffective or even counterproductive. As long as the Middle East’s highly militarized, zero-sum security dynamics remain, Ankara would be better off concentrating on interests closer to home that it has the power to protect.

This is not the first time that Turkey has tried to shape the broader Middle Eastern order. Ankara saw the Arab uprisings in the early 2010s as a historic window of opportunity. The ruling Justice and Development Party (AKP) described the political transformation sweeping the region as “the people’s coming to power,” and the Turkish government expected that eventual elections in the Arab countries rocked by protest would bring into office parties aligned with the Muslim Brotherhood, which was ideologically sympathetic and had practical links to the AKP. The emergence of friendly governments, the thinking went, would increase Turkey’s influence in the region.

Yet these hopes soon receded. Egypt’s Muslim Brotherhood–affiliated president, Mohamed Morsi, was overthrown in 2013, and Turkey’s bet on the opposition movement in Syria failed as Bashar al-Assad clung to power. Turkey was left isolated. A rival axis composed of Egypt, Saudi Arabia, and the UAE objected to Turkey’s Muslim Brotherhood ties and sought to limit Ankara’s influence. This group contested Turkey’s positions across the Middle East, North Africa, the eastern Mediterranean, and the Horn of Africa, effectively fighting proxy battles with Turkey in conflicts in Libya, Somalia, and Syria.

Then, the attacks on October 7, 2023, ushered in another period of upheaval. Some changes since have played in Turkey’s favor. Chief among them is the collapse of the Assad regime in Syria in December 2024 at the hands of opposition groups led by Hayat Tahrir al-Sham. Turkey had arguably invested more than any other country in pursuit of regime change in Damascus; it assisted opposition groups for years. Yet as the years went by, that objective seemed increasingly out of reach. The region’s other major powers made their peace with the regime’s survival, and even Ankara began considering rapprochement with Assad to avoid being left out in the cold. Assad’s fall changed Turkey’s fortunes overnight, handing it a level of influence in Syria it had not held in more than a decade.

Other developments proved more dangerous for Ankara. Israel’s foreign policy became more assertive and revisionist after October 7, and its expansive security objectives began to clash with Turkey’s own. Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan’s sharp criticism of Israeli policies in Gaza strained the relationship, and it deteriorated further when Turkey expanded its foothold in post-Assad Syria and Israel came to view Ankara’s military presence there as a direct threat. As Israel continues to strike sites in Syria—attacks Ankara has condemned—the risk of escalation is only growing. A miscalculation on either side could lead to a direct clash between Israeli and Turkish forces.

The U.S.-Israeli war with Iran, meanwhile, has cut both ways for Turkey. While Tehran’s attention has been focused elsewhere, Ankara has welcomed Iran’s retreat from Syria and the chance to push back against its remaining influence in Iraq. Turkey also has no interest in seeing a nuclear-armed Iran upend the regional balance of power. Yet the prospect of chaos across Turkey’s eastern border, should the Iranian regime fall, has bred real anxiety in Ankara, as has the possibility that Iran could become emboldened if the regime emerges from the war intact.

In this complex environment, Ankara has pursued a three-pronged strategy to protect its security interests and, it hopes, capitalize on opportunities the upheaval has created. Since U.S. President Donald Trump’s return to power, Erdogan has drawn Turkey closer to the United States and tried to use his relationship with Washington to advance Ankara’s regional policies. Turkey has also pressed ahead with regional normalization, building on efforts to repair ties with Saudi Arabia, Egypt, and other key Arab countries in the early 2020s, with the aim to forge a broader regional bloc. And, domestically, Ankara launched the “Turkey Without Terror” process, an attempt to resolve the conflict with the PKK inside and outside Turkey, which Turkey’s adversaries have long exploited by arming Kurdish groups or backing them politically.

Some of Ankara’s policies have borne fruit. Consider, for instance, the outreach to Washington. Erdogan’s close relations with Trump helped bring the United States on board with Turkey’s expanded role in Syria. Turkey was one of the four countries Washington brought in to help negotiate the U.S.-proposed October 2025 cease-fire agreement between Israel and Hamas, and as a result, Trump offered and Erdogan accepted a seat on the U.S. president’s so-called Board of Peace for Gaza’s reconstruction. Turkey has played an active part mediating between the United States and Iran during the latest war. And Trump publicly endorsed the Mecca Defense Alliance, calling it “big, bold, and important.” All of this points to a recognition in Washington of Turkey’s regional importance, the U.S. administration’s willingness to support some of Ankara’s priorities, and Turkey’s determination to elevate its own position.

Yet there are undeniable limits to Turkey’s quest for influence. At a February 2026 Board of Peace summit, for instance, Turkey was excluded from participating in a planned international troop deployment and police training in Gaza—a sign that warming relations with Washington were not enough to override Israeli objections to having a Turkish military footprint in the Palestinian territory. In Syria, similarly, the support Turkey has secured from the Trump administration has not stopped Israel from launching military strikes at Syrian targets near Turkish positions. Simply put, Turkey has not been able to use its relationship with the Trump administration to overcome the limit Israeli opposition places on the scope of Turkey’s regional role.

Turkey’s regional normalization efforts have yielded mixed results, too. Restoring diplomatic ties with significant players such as Saudi Arabia is in Turkey’s interest, to be sure. But the alliances Ankara is entering with these countries risk making Turkey and the region less secure, not more. The Mecca pact with Pakistan and Saudi Arabia could end up hardening rival blocs and elevating regional tensions. Turkey insists that the alliance is directed against no country in particular and has suggested that Egypt, Syria, or even Iran could eventually join. But in the meantime, Iran, Israel, and the Houthis could see it as a threat and try to test or weaken its members’ commitment.

Furthermore, the pact could pull Turkey into other countries’ wars. That possibility is already on the table now that the Turkish parliament has ratified the agreement and the Houthis are escalating their attacks on Saudi Arabia. The Turkish government has insisted that any military support it provided would be confined to deterrence, intelligence sharing, and defense-industrial cooperation. Yet Saudi Arabia is now seeking Pakistan’s and Turkey’s help with air defense and territorial protection, and the three states have agreed to prepare forces for rapid deployment. Sending Turkish troops to Saudi Arabia would still require separate parliamentary approval. If parliament allowed it, even a limited deployment would risk drawing Turkey into a wider regional conflict.

Some kind of regional security framework is necessary. The problem is that no country in the region, including Turkey, has the power to impose a security order on the rest, so whenever someone tries, other countries feel threatened. An informal cooperation framework that is open to all countries in the region—and that asks its members to commit to nonaggression instead of making mutual-defense pledges—might work better. But the assembly and functioning of such a coalition require trust that governments in the region do not have. Breaking the impasse would likely require the United States to play a more constructive role than it has in recent years—not simply endorsing whatever regional arrangement emerges, as Trump did with the Mecca pact, or pressuring countries to normalize relations with Israel without expecting Israel to change its policies in return, as Trump has also done. Instead, Washington, perhaps with the help of Beijing, would need to compel regional rivals such as Iran and Saudi Arabia to take confidence-building measures that would make an inclusive security framework viable in the long run. Without this external push, the narrower security blocs that emerge in the region will only lead to further instability.

Ankara’s strongest cards lie closer to home. The Kurdish peace process has moved slowly since February 2025, when Abdullah Ocalan, the imprisoned leader of the PKK, called on the militant group to disarm. But it has yielded tangible progress. In May 2025, the PKK announced that it would dissolve. In Syria, Kurdish forces are being integrated into the Syrian army, and Mazloum Abdi, the commander of the People’s Defense Units, a Kurdish armed group affiliated with the PKK, was appointed as a presidential adviser in August. Syria’s postconflict transition remains fragile, and the reconciliation between Damascus and Kurdish militants in the northeast may owe as much to American pressure as it does to Turkey’s domestic efforts to make peace with the PKK. But for Turkey, the eventual integration of Syrian Kurdish forces into the Syrian state removes the risk Ankara saw in having an autonomous, armed Kurdish entity on its southern border. And, given the close security and economic ties Ankara has developed with Damascus, as well as the political and legal steps the government can take at home to complete the disbanding of the PKK and to deepen democratic reforms, Turkey has the ability to keep these encouraging developments on track.

Turkey’s regional policies are most successful when the country plays to its strengths—not when it is trying to throw its weight around the region. In the first decade of the twenty-first century, Ankara built real influence by mediating between Syria and Israel and between Palestinian factions, expanding its economic engagement in the Levant, and proposing projects to connect the region’s energy and transportation infrastructure. Military power, meanwhile, has proved most effective when applied for specific purposes, such as to secure the border with Syria. This combination is Turkey’s winning formula: diplomacy and economic outreach to extend its influence, and limited force to protect its core interests. Broad, open-ended commitments, by contrast, risk drawing Turkey into regional conflicts without making it safer.

Today, sticking to a more effective formula does not mean retreating from regional politics. It means setting priorities and planning long-term. Consolidating the Kurdish peace process, keeping Washington invested in Syria’s stabilization, managing the risk of confrontation with Israel in Syria, and maintaining constructive relations with the Kurdistan Regional Government in northern Iraq and the Iraqi government in Baghdad are key to keeping Turkey’s borders secure and are within Turkey’s ability to achieve. Ankara can continue to use its experience in mediation when opportunities to advance peace processes present themselves. It can also promote economic connectivity, both by expanding trade ties and by supporting regional infrastructure projects such as the Iraq-Europe Development Road and a proposed pipeline network connecting the Gulf to Jordan, Syria, and Turkey. It need not shy away from security cooperation with the Gulf, either, if it can engage without making formal commitments. Yet in all these endeavors, Ankara should remember that it has the most clout when it stays out of the region’s conflicts.

The Middle East’s escalating wars and rival blocs could make a narrower Turkish security policy more difficult to sustain today than it was in the past. Defending Ankara’s interests and playing a constructive role in a volatile neighborhood will require a careful balance. The best way Turkey can contribute to regional stability may be to avoid trying to do too much.

The Overlooked Costs of a Transactional Foreign Policy

Even on the Sidelines, Ankara Faces Blowback

How Syria Can Capitalize on the Post-Hormuz Economy

Beijing’s Overreach Threatens the Détente With America

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan
