#5801. States rally behind Catholic church in first-of-its-kind Supreme Court fight over prayer shrine

First Liberty senior counsel Ryan Gardner explains to Fox News Digital why the Kentucky dispute could shape religious land-use protections nationwide.

FIRST ON FOX: A Catholic religious community's plans to build a small shrine to the Virgin Mary on its own property have triggered a years-long legal battle with two neighbors, drawing a coalition of state attorneys general into a potential landmark religious liberty showdown before the U.S. Supreme Court.

Kentucky Attorney General Russell Coleman is leading an 18-state coalition urging the justices to reverse a ruling that blocked the roughly 600-square-foot prayer grotto in Park Hills, Kentucky, despite the project initially receiving approval from local zoning officials.

The dispute pits the Missionaries of St. John the Baptist against neighboring property owners Joel and Elizabeth Frederic, who challenged the city's approval of the outdoor shrine and ultimately prevailed in Kentucky's appellate courts.

YOU SHOULDN’T NEED A PERMIT TO PRAY IN YOUR OWN HOME — THE SUPREME COURT SHOULD AGREE

St. John Church in Park Hills, Ky., is at the center of a Supreme Court case over plans to build a prayer grotto on its property. (First Liberty Institute)

"I’m proud to stand with other Attorney General colleagues from across the country in defense of these Kentuckians’ religious liberty," Coleman said in a statement provided to Fox News Digital. "This fundamental American freedom cannot be brushed away simply by a heckler’s veto, and I’m confident the justices will agree."

The coalition's intervention comes as the Supreme Court prepares to consider whether blocking a religious structure on an organization's property constitutes a "substantial burden" on religious exercise under the Religious Land Use and Institutionalized Persons Act (RLUIPA).

The case marks the first time the Supreme Court is considering the federal law's land-use protections, according to Coleman's office, raising the stakes for churches and religious organizations nationwide.

The controversy began in 2021 when the Missionaries sought permission to construct a 16-by-39-foot grotto honoring the Virgin Mary's apparition to Saint Bernadette in Lourdes, France.

SUPREME COURT SHOWDOWN LOOMS OVER ST MICHAEL STATUE BLOCKED FROM PUBLIC SAFETY BUILDING

The property near St. John Church in Park Hills, Ky., where the Missionaries of St. John the Baptist have sought to build a prayer grotto. (First Liberty Institute)

The proposed structure would include a shrine, plaza, walking path and retaining wall, offering a quiet place for prayer and meditation.

The church building has stood on the property for nearly a century, predating Park Hills' zoning ordinance adopted in 1974, according to previous Fox News Digital reporting.

The Park Hills Board of Adjustment approved the project in April 2021. But the Frederics challenged the approval in court, arguing that the proposed construction violated local zoning requirements.

A trial court initially upheld the city's decision before the Kentucky Court of Appeals reversed, finding that the zoning board exceeded its authority in granting the necessary permits.

SUPREME COURT TO HEAR CATHOLIC PARISH'S CHALLENGE AFTER COLORADO BARRED SCHOOLS FROM UNIVERSAL PRE-K PROGRAM

The Kentucky Supreme Court affirmed the rejection of the Missionaries' federal religious land-use claim in December 2025.

Coleman previously defended the religious organization's position before Kentucky's highest court and led a 20-state effort in April urging the U.S. Supreme Court to take up the dispute.

The justices agreed in October to hear the case, with oral arguments scheduled for Jan. 20, 2027.

Ryan Gardner, senior counsel at First Liberty Institute, which represents the Missionaries, previously told Fox News Digital that the stakes extend far beyond the proposed Catholic shrine.

"This is a massive case for religious liberty because it has the potential to impact every single zoning law in this country," Gardner said in an October Fox News Digital exclusive.

The Supreme Court is seen on Capitol Hill in Washington, Dec. 17, 2024. (J. Scott Applewhite, File/AP Photo)

Gardner compared the grotto's footprint to a swimming pool, underscoring the modest size of a project that has since reached the nation's highest court.

"RLUIPA is at the intersection of two very critical rights in this country: the rights of religious liberty and property rights," Gardner said.

Congress enacted RLUIPA in 2000 to protect religious institutions from certain burdens imposed through zoning and land-use regulations.

The Missionaries argue that preventing them from constructing a religious structure on their property substantially burdens their religious exercise.

The Frederics, however, have disputed that interpretation, arguing in their Supreme Court opposition brief that the religious organization failed to demonstrate why alternative locations for the grotto were unavailable.

They also challenged claims that lower courts are fundamentally divided over the legal standard for determining substantial burdens under RLUIPA.

The Supreme Court agreed to consider the substantial-burden question, rather than every issue raised in the underlying zoning dispute.

Kentucky Solicitor General Matt Kuhn, Principal Deputy Solicitor General Jack Heyburn and Deputy Solicitor General Jacob Abrahamson prepared the multistate brief, according to Coleman's office.

What began as a proposal for a quiet place to pray has become a national battle over religious liberty, property rights and how far neighbors can go in challenging religious construction on private property.

Jasmine Baehr is a breaking news writer for Fox News Digital, where she covers politics, the military, legal debates surrounding life and family policy, as well as faith and culture.

#5799. America’s Bad Bargain in Venezuela

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Javier Corrales is Dwight W. Morrow 1895 Professor of Political Science at Amherst College and the author of Autocracy Rising: How Venezuela Transitioned to Authoritarianism.

U.S. Dealmaking Is Strengthening the Chavista Regime

After the U.S. military captured then Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro in January, the United States set out on a mission to fix what President Donald Trump called a “dead country.” With broad authority over Venezuela’s agenda, officials in the Trump administration promised to free the country from socialism. As Secretary of State Marco Rubio said, when he was called before the Senate in late January, the administration’s ultimate objective was to establish “a friendly, stable, prosperous Venezuela, and democratic, in which all elements of society are represented in free and fair elections.”

Rubio laid out a three-stage framework for managing this democratic transition: stabilization, economic recovery, and, finally, transition. Washington is currently focused on the second stage, recovering the economy, but it is proceeding so quickly and with such little transparency that it is jeopardizing the stated end goal entirely. U.S. dealmaking with the country’s oil sector, in particular, is reifying rather than transforming Venezuela’s economic rentism: a model of governance in which the state controls important sectors of the economy and offers business contracts and market privileges to firms that support its agenda. This is precisely the system that facilitated the Venezuelan state’s rampant corruption over the past 25 years, allowing autocratic leaders to drown out democratic forces and win elections.

As it pursues its economic goals with dizzying speed, the Trump administration is also slow-walking democratization efforts. For months, Washington excluded opposition leader María Corina Machado and her party, Vente Venezuela, from any direct involvement in negotiations. Machado had left the country last December to receive a Nobel Peace Prize after nearly 20 months in hiding, and after Maduro’s removal, the United States did little to help her return. Venezuela’s pro-democracy forces have thus been left feeling like they are pushing the regime in Caracas to democratize with the acquiescence, but not necessarily the full enthusiasm, of the United States.

Last month, Machado returned to Venezuela, and now she has the potential to change the game. Machado could become the greatest asset or, possibly, the greatest disruptor to Venezuela’s democratic transition. If Machado coordinates with the faction of the opposition that is currently negotiating with the government, any outcome will be seen as legitimate. But if she opposes and speaks out against the result, Venezuelans will find themselves in a familiar and dangerous place: with a divided opposition, a government with few constraints on its power, and a transition in purgatory.

So far, Washington has squandered its immense leverage over Caracas. By dragging out the transition and pursuing questionable economic deals, Washington is endangering not only its own end goal but also the greatest opportunity Venezuela has had to overcome more than a quarter century of autocratic governance.

During Trump’s first term, his administration centered its criticism of Maduro on the country’s socialist model. In February 2019, weeks after recognizing the opposition leader Juan Guaidó as Venezuela’s interim president, Trump promised in a speech that the “twilight hour of socialism has arrived in our hemisphere.” That campaign to oust Maduro failed, but Trump’s diagnosis of what ails Venezuela was wrong to begin with. Over the past two decades, Venezuela has not been operating primarily under a socialist model but an extractivist one. And since January 3, the United States has seemed only to reinforce it.

Venezuela is an oil state. Under Chavismo, the ideology named for Maduro’s predecessor Hugo Chávez, the state used oil revenues as a political slush fund to reward allies. Little went toward investments in the oil sector or the rest of the economy. Secret deals with oil magnates at home and abroad guided most governance decisions. Whereas the government’s critics faced legal attacks, the well-connected received impunity. Chávez implemented elements of socialism, of course, with massive nationalizations and price controls, but the key priority was collusion and special deals. Many of the state’s allies were poor Venezuelans whose votes kept the autocrats in power, but less noticed were the economic elites. These private actors shunned opposition figures and refrained from any public criticism of the regime; in exchange, they accumulated regulatory favors, access to preferential exchange rates, or exclusive contracts with the state. This model even applied internationally; around 2007, Chávez began redirecting oil sales from the United States to China, both to reward China for being so friendly toward the regime and to preempt a possible U.S. divestment from the Venezuelan oil sector. Oil booms in the first decade of the century allowed the country’s populist leaders to build vertical patronage systems that rewarded loyalty and starved out dissent.

Eventually, however, this system ran its course, leaving Venezuela’s oil industry in shambles. In the 1990s, before Chávez took power, oil production hovered around 3.5 million barrels per day; by 2019, oil production had dropped to 700,000 to 800,000 barrels per day. The government has recognized this dismal state; acting President Delcy Rodríguez has said that the oil sector will need as much as $76 billion in external capital to recover over the next five years.

The United States is a plausible partner for Venezuela’s oil recovery. Washington is responsive to high domestic and global demand for oil, especially since the war in Iran has disrupted flows from the Middle East. American oil firms have the resources to rebuild Venezuelan production and, just as important, the refineries needed to process Venezuelan oil, which is among the world’s heaviest and most sulfurous.

But rather than replacing, or at least revamping, this Chavista system, the United States seems to have embraced its structure. In August, the U.S. government struck an agreement with North American Blue Energy Partners (NABEP), a private Venezuelan corporation, that Trump called “the biggest oil deal in the history of the world.” According to the White House fact sheet of the deal, the Venezuelan state granted NABEP a 100-year concession to operate 17 oil fields, which could cover one-fifth of Venezuela’s total proven reserves. The company is led by Alejandro Betancourt, a Venezuelan citizen being investigated in Spain and Switzerland for money laundering and widely regarded as having collaborated with Chávez and acting as an intermediary between Trump and Rodríguez. Meanwhile, the agreement gives the U.S. Defense Department a 35 percent stake in NABEP, and the State Department gets the right to buy 20 percent of NABEP’s oil at the cost of production—meaning NABEP makes no profit on that share—and has preferential access, at market prices, to the rest.

Outside of these details, little is known about the arrangement or how it came to be. There was no competitive public bidding on the fields—a practice that is generally recommended every time governments assign contracts to private and foreign actors—and there was no explanation of how Washington will manage the export proceeds or clarity on who will fund the necessary investments. The U.S. Congress has not reviewed the deal, although Democrats, with a new majority starting in January, have promised to investigate it.

This deal is also wildly unpopular across Venezuela’s political spectrum. Although Venezuelans have generally celebrated the return of U.S. capital to the country, nearly 80 percent of Venezuelans did not support the oil deal, according to a poll released in September by Meganálisis. Even Machado, who rarely criticizes the United States, has expressed reservations, saying that Betancourt has been responsible for “obscene corruption.” Hardcore leftists see the deal as capitalist robbery. Nationalists see it as neoimperialism. Democratic forces see it as a bailout for the dictatorship. Ordinary citizens see it as a missed opportunity to spread Venezuela’s wealth more equitably.

These oil and gas deals could generate an oil boom if investment comes quickly, production capacity is restored, competitive bidding expands, and government spending and welfare institutions are reformed. But as Trump said when celebrating the deal at the United Nations in September, “To the victor belong the spoils,” and the victors, in this case, are Washington, the ruling party, and a private businessman.

The Venezuelan state has also shown little interest in helping the Venezuelan people and directing petrodollars toward human capital or public welfare. Instead, it will likely do what Chavismo perfected in previous booms: bankroll patronage networks, buy off corporate allies, deny funds to competitors, and spend on electoral campaigns. These new deals could very well end up financing the ruling party’s political rebirth.

The rest of the Venezuelan economy’s recovery, meanwhile, remains a mystery. There have been no efforts toward establishing universal property rights, modernizing contract law, reforming the financial sector, or dismantling state monopolies. Without such inclusive economic rights and democratic rules, oil windfalls seldom produce equitable growth. They produce skewed gains for the state and its friends.

The White House sees the deal as locking Venezuela into a secure economic relationship with the United States for at least 100 years. This week, he referred to the country as “our new acquisition.” But this might be a false sense of security. The agreement is so distasteful to so many Venezuelans that, rather than inoculate Venezuela against anti-imperialist socialism, it is making a radical backlash more likely.

Almost a year after Maduro’s departure, very few concrete steps have been taken to organize a free and fair election in Venezuela. Instead, the Rodríguez government and an opposition delegation led by Dinorah Figuera, who is president of Venezuela’s exiled National Assembly in Spain, held a series of talks this fall. The parties did make progress, including agreeing to unblock certain banned websites and to allow the government to reclaim sanctioned gold reserves held by the Bank of England. Most significantly, after months of pressure from the Venezuelan opposition, the Venezuelan government agreed in August to replace all 32 judges in the Supreme Tribunal of Justice, accepting nominations from civil society that included the opposition. This is a gigantic baby step. In Venezuela, the Supreme Tribunal of Justice long served at the center of Venezuela’s famous autocratic legalism. It was the regime’s preeminent weapon to disqualify opposition candidates and elected officials, accepting laws that justified repression, refusing to investigate extrajudicial killings, and persecuting opposition figures on fabricated charges. An independent judiciary could invalidate current laws that criminalize dissent and establish new ones to promote fair electoral rules. Establishing even a modest framework for an independent judiciary is indispensable for any transition to minimal democracy.

And yet committing to reform the judiciary, however important, is not enough. A full transition to democracy will require far more: a new constitution with robust institutional checks and balances, new laws promoting civil liberties, and more oversight of intelligence and security organizations, which have been known to engage in arbitrary detentions and torture. This will also require retraining the law and order apparatus, namely the National Police and the military’s internal security forces, the Bolivarian National Guard, to respect civil rights; redesigning the electoral system and naming independent electoral authorities; and rebuilding civil society and a free press. Judicial reform in isolation will not dismantle an entrenched autocratic regime.

This week, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced an expansion of U.S.-Venezuelan military cooperation, mostly targeted at fighting crime. But fighting crime effectively does not necessarily require more brute force but rather good intelligence, a robust criminal justice system, a professional and law-abiding police force, and investments in community building, namely in strengthening ties of trust between neighbors and police. It is not clear that these elements will be part of this new U.S.-Venezuelan security cooperation. Unless new agreements come with commitments for internal state reform, the United States risks helping a dictatorship acquire even more coercive power.

Most important, the transition process has ignored the will of the people. Most Venezuelans want a fair election, plain and simple. In a Meganálisis poll conducted in April, 87 percent of respondents agreed that the “solution for Venezuela is a presidential election in 2026.” Even with so much material deprivation—inflation is in the triple digits, and close to 70 percent of the population still lives in poverty—the people want to vote first.

Rubio and Rodríguez have argued that stabilization is necessary before elections can be held. But there is reason to believe that elections are not the result of stability but the source of stability. Elections produce legitimacy for authorities. The current authorities not only aided the suppression of the last election, in 2024, but they have also passed the limit of 180 days that an interim government can serve, according to Venezuela’s constitution. Any policy that it advances is, thus, also less legitimate than those put in place by an elected government.

Moreover, Machado’s absence from the process so far risks making any major deal between the government and the opposition fragile. She is the most popular politician in the country and the leader of the largest pro-democracy movement that Latin America has seen since the first decade of this century. Across multiple polls, including by Meganálisis and AtlasIntel, she has approval ratings between 57 and 76, compared with Rodríguez’s single digits, and multiple international accounts have shown that her substitute in the 2024 election, Edmundo González, easily won. This popularity makes Machado a threat to the Chavista regime, and her exclusion likely came at least in part as a request by the Rodríguez government.

Rather than inaugurating a democratic and economic renaissance, the United States is potentially enabling the survival of a rentist regime. During the oil boom of the first two decades of this century, the Chavista regime insulated itself from domestic accountability by exchanging oil rents for votes and elite support. China served as its primary geopolitical patron, unconcerned with accountability so long as the crude flowed eastward. The United States’ playbook today eerily imitates this model. Venezuela’s main external partner has now changed, but that partner has similar functions. By prioritizing narrow resource extraction over deep institutional reform, the United States has opted to become the senior partner in a rentier compact. Washington is Venezuela’s new toll collector.

Thus far, Venezuela’s democratic transition has delivered only a commitment to reform the Supreme Tribunal of Justice. Nearly a year after Maduro’s ousting, there is no election calendar, no clear plan to reform the electoral system, and no meaningful internal changes to how the government generates and spends its revenues. Despite all the leverage Trump claims to have over the Venezuelan government, outside of business deals, he has very little to show for it.

If this trajectory persists, the United States will not propel a transition to democracy, market principles, and the rule of law in Venezuela. It will instead re-create the very conditions that produced Venezuela’s autocracy in the first place.

#5798. Dem lawmaker to resign after report says he made 'unwelcome' sexual advances toward staffer

Trey Gowdy addresses the persistent problem of sexual impropriety on Capitol Hill during 'Sunday Night in America.'

A West Coast Democrat announced his resignation from the House of Representatives after their bipartisan internal watchdog stated that he could have committed criminal sexual assault against congressional staffers on multiple occasions.

An explosive report by the House Ethics Committee released on Monday claimed that Rep. Jimmy Gomez, D-Calif., violated congressional rules on sexual misconduct by making "unwelcome" advances toward a staffer for the House, disregarding the power imbalance between lawmakers and aides in the process.

The report also went a step further, warning Gomez could have violated existing law.

Gomez has since announced his intent to resign on Dec. 1.

HOUSE DEMOCRAT INVESTIGATED OVER ALLEGED 'INAPPROPRIATE SEXUAL CONTACT' WITH STAFFER

Rep. Jimmy Gomez speaks during a news conference on affordability on Capitol Hill in Washington, D.C., on Dec. 18, 2025. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

The bipartisan panel found that "Representative Gomez knew or had reason to know that his contact was not welcome" when out with the staffer, called "Witness 1," at a D.C. bar.

"There is also evidence that he knew or had reason to know his repeated advance towards Witness 1 in his congressional office was inappropriate and unwelcome. Accordingly, Representative Gomez’s conduct towards Witness 1 could implicate D.C. laws related to criminal sexual assault, including misdemeanor sexual abuse," the report said.

SWALWELL ANNOUNCES RESIGNATION FROM CONGRESS AFTER SEXUAL MISCONDUCT ALLEGATIONS TORPEDOED GUBERNATORIAL BID

Gomez has not been charged with any crime and has maintained his innocence. Fox News Digital reached out to his office for comment but did not immediately receive a response.

Gomez was a close ally of disgraced former Rep. Eric Swalwell, D-Calif., who resigned after being accused of sexual assault — which he denied. Gomez is also founder of the pro-family Congressional Dads Caucus.

The bipartisan ethics panel announced in August that it was investigating Gomez over allegations including "inappropriate sexual contact with a House staffer."

Gomez told Fox News Digital at the time that he "made personal mistakes outside my marriage" but added that his extramarital relations were consensual and that he did not break any laws.

CAPITOL HILL SEX SCANDALS TRIGGER NEW PUSH TO REDRAW THE LINE FOR LAWMAKERS

But the lawyers for one of the women interviewed by the committee disputed that in comments to Fox News Digital, accusing the congressman of sexual assault.

Monday’s report shows the committee appears to support her side of the story, and that Gomez’s actions "at various junctures suggests he knew that what he was doing was wrong."

The staffer is identified as having worked for a congressional leadership office, rather than on Gomez’s own staff.

Before coming out with her accusations, the staffer testified to the committee that she did not want Gomez "to feel upset in any way," and that she was "afraid that I was going to get in trouble at work," the report said.

"Even if Representative Gomez believed that his actions were wholly consensual, his failure to appreciate the reality of this power dynamic and thus the untenable position he put a young congressional staffer in, is inconsistent with the standards required of a Member of Congress," the report said.

The investigation into Gomez began after reports surfaced that he had been seen kissing a congressional staffer at a party attended by lawmakers and House employees.

Rep. Jimmy Gomez, a Democrat from California, sits on the House steps at the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., on July 3, 2025. (Kent Nishimura/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Gomez initially disclosed two relationships involving congressional staffers, describing one as a past consensual sexual relationship and the other as "one sole occasion" of "consensual kissing."

GOP LAWMAKER'S GIFTS, 'EFFUSIVE AFFECTION' TOWARD YOUNG STAFFERS DETAILED IN EXPLOSIVE ETHICS REPORT

However, investigators found credible allegations that Gomez made unwanted sexual advances more than once toward the second woman, Witness 1, which the woman claimed was "nonconsensual whatsoever."

The woman, who was in her mid-20s at the time, testified that Gomez put his hand underneath her skirt and penetrated her with his fingers without consent during a February 2024 gathering at a bar in D.C., and testified to investigators that she fled to the bathroom crying after the incident.

Gomez defended himself against the allegation, telling the committee that the woman "did not say no," and did not "push" him away, while denying penetrating her.

Investigators described a second encounter with the woman in July 2024, claiming that Gomez grabbed her as she attempted to leave his congressional office, pulled her toward him and tried to kiss her.

She described Gomez as "[p]retty forceful" and said she repeatedly tried to push him away, the report said.

Gomez denied the allegation, maintaining that he merely hugged the woman and kissed her on the cheek.

Investigators then refuted his defense after they examined text messages suggesting Gomez was concerned about being seen with the woman by members of his staff, noting that he switched their communications to Signal and enabled disappearing messages. The committee also noted that Gomez initially disclosed only consensual kissing before later acknowledging sexual contact during his testimony.

After reviewing documents and speaking with 10 witnesses, the committee concluded that Gomez violated clauses 1 and 18(b) of the House Code of Official Conduct, which prohibit unwelcome sexual advances toward House employees and require lawmakers to conduct themselves in a manner reflecting creditably on the House.

Investigators concluded that Gomez's conduct caused the woman significant emotional distress and contributed to her decision to leave congressional employment in 2025.

"The Committee reiterates its commitment to investigating allegations and providing accountability to those who face sexual misconduct from Members and their staff. Here, the House lost Witness 1 as a competent and able staffer because of Representative Gomez’s misconduct and because of a broader culture of disrespect and toxic sexual behavior toward staff that permeates life on the Hill and is long overdue for correction," the report said.

A slew of lawmakers on both sides have been accused of sexual assault or harassment over the last year, prompting a reckoning over how Congress protects staffers from predatory work environments.

#5795. Congress and the Coming Crises

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

IVO H. DAALDER is Senior Fellow at Harvard’s Belfer Center for Science and International Affairs. He served as U.S. Ambassador to NATO from 2009 to 2013.

JAMES M. LINDSAY is Mary and David Boies Distinguished Senior Fellow in U.S. Foreign Policy at the Council on Foreign Relations.

How to Defuse Looming Threats to U.S. National Security

Democrats won a historic victory in the U.S. midterm elections by running on a key voter concern: affordability. They promised to lower gas and diesel prices, curb electricity and water bills, and cut borrowing costs. Now voters expect results.

Making progress will not be easy, given that the affordability crisis is inextricably linked to a set of thorny policy issues. Soaring gas and diesel prices stem from wars in Europe and the Middle East that have overextended the United States and provided China, Iran, North Korea, and Russia openings to exploit. Rising electricity and water bills are driven by the growing number of data centers powering an artificial intelligence revolution that is nearing a point of no return, when machines improve faster than humans can understand or control. And higher prices and borrowing costs are the result of an unsustainable U.S. national debt, now above $40 trillion, that requires exorbitant interest payments and has foreign governments questioning the safety of the dollar and U.S. Treasury bonds.

These problems not only strain Americans’ pocketbooks but also threaten the security and prosperity of the United States—and the world. If Beijing and Moscow try to take advantage of a weakened United States by escalating simultaneously in Taiwan and eastern Europe, those moves could be the first steps toward World War III. If AI escapes the bounds of human control, no one will be safe. And if the national debt continues to soar, Washington could trigger a fiscal crisis that devastates the American economy and leaves the United States far less able to meet the challenges it faces overseas.

Action to address the United States’ problems therefore cannot wait. But politics may stand in the way. The White House is more likely to obstruct than to cooperate with congressional Democrats over the next two years, as President Donald Trump’s appointment of the pugilistic Stephen Miller as the new White House chief of staff underscores. Moreover, one electoral victory did not erase the country’s deep partisan and policy divisions. Going into the 2028 presidential election, Republicans know that a successful Democratic Congress would endanger their chances of keeping the White House. Trump and congressional Republicans, therefore, have considerable motivation to derail Democratic plans.

Democrats are not without options, however. They can pursue legislation, seeking cross-party collaboration when possible and pushing ahead even in the face of presidential vetoes. Sometimes it is the president, not Congress, who blinks. When he doesn’t, voters will know who stood in the way. Democrats can also use public hearings and the investigative powers of Congress to shed light on the threats facing the country and to build a case for change—much as the Fulbright hearings did during the Vietnam War and the Church Committee did to highlight Cold War–era intelligence abuses. They can put the defense secretary and military commanders, AI executives, and Treasury and budget officials under oath to account for how they prosecuted the Iran war, raced ahead on AI without adequate safeguards, and exploded the deficit. They can also call on economists, scientists, and strategists to explain how to steer the country away from peril.

Congressional efforts may not be enough to meaningfully change Trump’s policies. But the risks of major war, unconstrained AI, and eroding economic competitiveness are too great to wait two more years to address. These problems, and the affordability crisis they have generated, will only get worse. If Democrats in Congress do not start advancing a serious agenda to address them now, these dangers may become impossible to contain.

The first order of the new Congress’s business should be to end the Iran war. Operation Epic Fury has sent energy prices soaring and put freedom of navigation at risk. It has also highlighted the United States’ vulnerabilities in ways that could prove dangerous—and costly—in the years ahead. Iranian missiles and drones wreaked havoc on American military bases in the Persian Gulf. U.S. efforts to defeat these attacks depleted supplies of critical munitions, showing that the U.S. military has become overly reliant on very expensive, hard-to-produce high-tech weapons. The economic, diplomatic, military, and intelligence support that China and Russia have provided Iran revealed the extent to which autocracies are cooperating to stretch U.S. capabilities and undermine American power. The U.S. Navy is effectively bottled up in the Arabian Sea, limiting its presence in the Atlantic and Pacific. In theory, U.S. allies could help fill the gaps, but Trump’s decision to launch the war without consulting them and his adversarial attitude toward them have strained those relationships, weakening deterrence of a Russia and China bent on securing strategic advantage.

Perceptions of weakness, even when they are wrong, are often why wars begin. Moscow and Beijing might see the next two years as a window of opportunity. Europe and Japan are rearming, but that buildup will take years. The American military is stretched thin, and political divisions make decisive action difficult. Together this could be an invitation for U.S. adversaries to engage in aggression in Europe and Asia. Russia is already pursuing a shadow war against NATO and could test the alliance’s unity further by probing the Baltic states. China could impose a blockade on Taiwan. It is not difficult to see how, if Moscow and Beijing take these steps in concert, the resulting conflicts could escalate into a global war.

The new Congress will have limited ability to forestall these events. The U.S. Constitution puts much of diplomacy beyond the reach of the legislature; only the president decides when to negotiate, what to discuss, and with whom. But Congress does have tools to wield. If the Trump administration remains in a standoff with the Iranian regime when Democrats take control in January, Congress should immediately invoke the War Powers Resolution to pass binding legislation demanding that Trump either secure congressional authorization for the war or end it. Congress can also use its power of the purse to condition or halt further spending on the war, preferably as part of must-pass legislation, such as a defense appropriations bill, to avoid a potential Senate filibuster. Trump would likely veto any such legislation, and Congress has a poor record on overriding presidential vetoes. But challenging a war that Americans clearly oppose would fulfill the promise Democrats made on the campaign trail, reduce the likelihood that Trump would escalate in dangerous ways, and mark a step forward in Congress’s reclamation of its war powers.

There is more the Democratic Congress can do to lower the risks of global conflict, too. It can start by taking a stronger line on Russia. Moscow has repaid Trump’s conciliatory policies—including his waiving of sanctions Congress authorized earlier this year with the bipartisan Lindsay O. Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act—with aid to Tehran and an intensified war on Ukraine. Only greater pressure can force the Kremlin to stand down. Congress should amend the Graham Act to give itself the right to review and overturn any presidential waivers of sanctions mandated under the law. It should also reaffirm the current ban on energy purchases from Russia and prohibit the administration from using government funds to follow through on its proposal to restart Russian gas and oil exports to Europe through a U.S. intermediary. Congress should resume military aid to Ukraine, ensure the availability of Starshield communications to Ukrainian forces, and refuse to fund any reduction in the permanent U.S. military presence in Europe or Asia that it has not approved itself.

Finally, to help dissuade China from threatening Taiwan, Congress should use defense spending bills to speed up the sale of arms to Taiwan. And Congress should legally codify the 1982 Six Assurances, which include a promise by Washington not to consult Beijing on arms sales to Taiwan. Many of these measures could garner sufficient Republican support to pass with veto-proof majorities, forcing Trump’s hand.

Additionally, Congress should use its oversight powers to assess the vulnerabilities that the Iran war has revealed. It should evaluate whether the U.S. intelligence agencies and the Pentagon adequately anticipated Iran’s response to U.S. attacks, the scale and causes of the shortages of high-tech weapons, and the risks that AI-aided drone warfare poses to U.S. overseas bases. Congress can also review the extent of Chinese and Russian support for Iran; assess NATO’s defenses against Russia, including its response to the escalating shadow war; determine U.S. readiness for Chinese efforts to coerce Taiwan; and evaluate proposals to make Pentagon acquisition more flexible and less expensive. An essential step toward enhancing U.S. deterrence is to identify what is broken in the U.S. defense posture and what needs to be fixed.

The new Congress will also need to rein in the AI industry. Opposition to AI data centers and other infrastructure galvanized voters in the midterms. Across the country, 46 of 47 ballot measures passed, placing new restrictions on new data centers, which have brought rising power and water bills and explosive land costs. Many Americans fear what AI will do to jobs today and for the next generation. They distrust the tech executives who control it and question who will truly benefit from its development.

Those concerns are justified. AI is advancing with increasing speed and introducing unknown risks. Five months ago, AI generated one percent of Anthropic’s research and development; today, it accounts for more than a quarter. Lab leaders predict that AI will become self-generating within a year. That is a scary prospect, not only for displaced computer scientists but also for society at large. AI could pose an existential threat to global computer networks; rogue AI agents have already colluded to evade human control. Leading firms have warned of even greater dangers. Alphabet said recently that its products could lead to “severe or catastrophic security and societal incidents,” and Anthropic has cited “catastrophic or existential risks to humanity.”

AI also poses profound national security challenges for the United States. The Trump administration and the tech industry have invoked an innovation race with China as a reason not to regulate the technology. That race is real, but many of the most important stages are not the ones Washington is fixating on. They are also ones Beijing is winning. Perhaps U.S. firms will be the first to develop artificial general intelligence. But in the meantime, the rest of the world and many U.S. companies are adopting Chinese open-weight AI models. Alibaba’s Qwen model now accounts for more than half of all open-source model downloads worldwide. As cheap, easy-to-adapt Chinese models become the global default, Beijing gains leverage over the digital infrastructure on which other countries’ economies and militaries will run. The companies that build these models are legally bound to cooperate with Chinese intelligence services. And because these models spread with few safeguards, they are easier to turn to malicious use.

Meanwhile, AI is transforming war. Autonomous weapons are essential to Ukraine’s defense against a superior military, shooting down long-range drones and targeting Russian forces, supply lines, and strategic industries. This year, the Pentagon quietly revised its doctrine to let AI select and initiate strikes while humans merely monitor, which risks errors and escalation at a speed no human can catch. And as AI lowers the barriers to entry for launching cyberattacks and developing new biological weapons, every country is in danger.

The Trump administration is clearly reluctant to address any of these threats. It is beholden to figures such as Jensen Huang, Elon Musk, and Mark Zuckerburg who have wanted to keep the AI industry unregulated and unconstrained. Huang and Nvidia, the company he led, had long pushed the administration to relax U.S. export controls that restricted the sale of AI chips to China and other countries; the firm’s current scandal has revealed just how loose those restrictions had become. Now, the administration wants China to report serious AI-related incidents through a new bilateral channel yet opposes requiring American companies to do the same. Given how rapidly AI is advancing, Congress cannot afford to stand idly by. There is a slew of legislative proposals already circulating, many of them with bipartisan support, that would require incident reporting, auditors in AI labs, and mandatory safety testing before deployment. Other AI industry leaders, such as Anthropic’s Dario Amodei and Alphabet’s Demis Hassabis, have supported these ideas, but the Trump administration has ignored them, asking instead for the industry to police itself.

Finding the right regulations and safety mechanisms, and then codifying them in legislation, will take time. In the meantime, Congress should use its convening power to seek input from industry leaders, experts, and the public about how this technology can be controlled. The leaders of frontier AI labs should be asked, under oath, to explain not just the upside of AI but also its risks. Elon Musk, for example, told The Economist this summer that within ten years, work will be “optional” because of AI—but he has also said there is a ten to 20 percent chance that AI will lead to the extinction of humanity. He and others who share this belief should be forced to justify why this risk is acceptable and why tech leaders are the ones to decide on behalf of humanity. They should also explain what it would take to reduce the ten to 20 percent chance to zero.

Public hearings that expose both the dangers and the opportunities of AI will make it easier to galvanize action to harness the technology’s promise safely while preventing the worst possible outcomes. And Congress has some levers it can pull now to address threats to national security. It can use the defense authorization bill to require that humans retain meaningful control over lethal force and to demand an accounting of how AI is reshaping military doctrine. It can also fund efforts to make American models, rather than Chinese ones, the default choice for countries around the world. If legislation takes too long to pass, hearings can at least lay the groundwork for a future administration to act.

Finally, Congress must address threats to the United States’ economic power. With $40 trillion in public debt, an annual budget deficit of $2 trillion, and annual interest payments topping more than $1 trillion, the U.S. economy is living on borrowed time. China and Russia are working to “de-dollarize” their economies by encouraging the use of cryptocurrency and other means of settling international transactions and by creating alternatives to the U.S.-dominated global banking system.

Even U.S. allies are reducing their reliance on that system: Norway’s sovereign wealth fund has cut its holding of U.S. dollars, the Dutch and French central banks have pulled their gold holdings from New York, and European Central Bank President Christine Lagarde has called for a “global euro” that would serve as an alternative to the dollar. If the dollar were to lose its status as the world’s premier reserve currency, Washington would be forced to spend even more money servicing the debt than it does now, intensifying the country’s fiscal problems, slowing economic growth while driving up inflation, and diminishing its ability to use economic and financial sanctions to pressure adversaries.

An immediate objective for the Democratic Congress will be to rein in Trump’s tariffs, because they raise prices, unsettle the markets that finance U.S. debt, and make U.S. producers less competitive globally without making a meaningful dent in the deficit. Congress will be operating from a disadvantage. Over the past century, it has delegated to the White House considerable tariff-setting authority, which Trump has eagerly exercised. Clawing back that power will be difficult. Significant disagreement exists among Democrats and between Democrats and Republicans about how to rewrite tariff law, and Trump will likely fight to defend his favorite foreign policy tool. Yet one area where concerted congressional action might be possible is in walking back Trump’s unilateral tariffs on Canada, which not only run counter to the U.S.-Mexico-Canada trade agreement that Trump negotiated and Congress approved in his first term but also hurt Republican voters in Maine and across the Midwest. Congress can aim to repeal Section 338 of the Tariff Act of 1930, which allows the president to impose tariffs of up to 50 percent on countries that treat U.S. goods differently from those from other countries. The provision was a forgotten relic until Trump invoked it in August to impose a fresh round of tariffs on Canada. And legislators should narrow the reach of other presidential tariff authorities by mandating expedited congressional review, instituting joint resolutions of disapproval that cannot be filibustered, and adding sunset provisions to emergency tariff authorities so that the president needs congressional approval to keep them in place.

Tariffs, however, are only part of the problem. A comprehensive solution to the country’s fiscal troubles would require raising taxes or cutting entitlements, which neither party has been willing to do. But Democrats have other cards they can play to start undoing the damage of the past two years. Trump’s 2025 One Big Beautiful Bill Act will cost the Treasury some $4.5 trillion over the next decade; Congress should repeal the law’s tax breaks for corporations and the wealthiest Americans and restore the Internal Revenue Service’s ability to collect taxes that are already owed. It should also write the Federal Reserve’s independence into law, so that no president can force interest rates down to make government borrowing cheaper. And Congress should establish a bipartisan commission modeled on the 1981 Greenspan Commission, which resolved a Social Security funding crisis, to recommend steps the United States must take to put its finances on a sustainable path and to ensure its global economic competitiveness.

In his 1948 campaign, Harry Truman salvaged his presidency by running against what he called the “do-nothing” Congress. That history should be a warning to Democrats. Voters handed them a congressional majority to bring down costs, and they must start right away to address the forces driving those costs—wars stretching American power to the breaking point, an AI revolution racing beyond human control, and a fiscal hole eroding the foundations of U.S. economic power. If the Democratic majority spends the next two years relitigating the administration’s misdeeds but failing to make progress on the issue voters care most about, Trump and the next Republican presidential candidate will be happy to brand it the do-nothing Congress of 2028.

Passing bills that can become law, forcing the president to veto those that cannot, and using hearings to show Americans what is at stake may still fall short. Ending and averting war, regulating a powerful tech industry, and protecting the United States’ economic competitiveness are difficult for Congress to do without presidential cooperation. But two years without any progress would only leave the affordability problem in place and the United States in a more dangerous global position than it is in now. A Congress that confronts these problems directly can at least make headway. And if it does its job right, it will hand the next president an agenda equal to the moment.

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

#5794. Bessent gave Iran weeks before it had ‘nothing left to trade.’ New sanctions expose what survived

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent joins 'Sunday Morning Futures' to discuss the Trump administration's economic sanctions against Iran, predicting a swift collapse of the regime's economy. Bessent also addresses the Iranian death threats that forced his family to relocate to a military base and weighs in on the compliance failures at Nvidia amid its ongoing espionage probe.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent warned in September that Iran would have "nothing left to trade" within two weeks.

Nearly two months later, the Trump administration is still targeting the overseas financial networks Tehran has used to keep money moving despite sweeping U.S. sanctions in the midst of ongoing war.

On Monday, Treasury announced another round of sanctions targeting more than two dozen individuals and companies accused of helping Iran move billions of dollars through financial networks spanning Dubai, Hong Kong and Turkey.

Bessent has repeatedly warned that Iran is nearing economic collapse, describing successive crackdowns as steps toward cutting off the regime's remaining financial lifelines. Yet each new round of sanctions has exposed more of the networks Tehran has used to keep doing business abroad — even as mounting economic pressure has failed to produce a diplomatic breakthrough.

COULD IRAN’S REGIME COLLAPSE? EXPERTS ASSESS BESSENT’S BID TO SEVER FINANCIAL LIFELINES

Secretary Scott Bessent speaks to members of the media outside the White House in Washington, D.C., on Oct. 22, 2025 (Aaron Schwartz/CNP/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

While the administration says its blockade has largely stopped Iranian oil exports, Monday's sanctions detail how Tehran has relied on currency dealers, trading companies and middlemen to conduct business abroad.

Treasury identified approximately $14.7 billion in financial activity potentially linked to Iranian shadow banking between January 2025 and August 2026, underscoring the scale of the networks Washington is now targeting.

Just three days earlier, Treasury declared that Iran had exhausted its seaborne oil supply outside the U.S. blockade, saying no new oil had been loaded onto vessels since the launch of Operation Economic Outcast in August.

"The regime has exhausted its seaborne oil supply, and it may never see the revenue from the oil it has already sold. The ship is sinking," Bessent said Friday.

In a response to Fox News Digital, Treasury said Iran has not loaded a single barrel of oil onto vessels since Aug. 25, 2026, and claimed Tehran is struggling to access foreign currency and recover proceeds from previous oil sales.

The department also provided a report showing the Iranian rial has lost roughly 25% of its value since August, while inflation reached 90% in September. Treasury said it is continuing to target alternative financial channels, including cryptocurrency exchanges, as Tehran looks for ways around U.S. restrictions.

BESSENT SAYS IRAN WILL HAVE 'NOTHING LEFT TO TRADE' WITHIN TWO WEEKS AS OIL LIFELINE DRIES UP

A cargo ship sails in the Persian Gulf toward the Strait of Hormuz, April 22, 2026. (AP Photo)

But even as the administration claims success in cutting off new oil shipments, Treasury is still pursuing Iranian operatives accused of collecting billions of dollars from previous sales. Friday's sanctions targeted four individuals involved in recovering oil revenues held overseas, some of which Treasury said had been diverted by middlemen for personal gain.

Bessent's September prediction was hardly his first warning that Tehran was approaching an economic breaking point.

Months earlier, he said U.S. sanctions were "asphyxiating" Iran's economy and warned that the country could soon be forced to shut down oil wells as storage facilities filled up.

By late August, Bessent was predicting Iran's economy could collapse within "weeks or months," as the administration launched Operation Economic Outcast, a campaign aimed at cutting off the regime's remaining revenue.

The warnings continued in October, when Bessent projected Iran's economy could shrink by more than 25% over the year ending in March 2027. Earlier this month, he described the country as experiencing one of the fastest economic collapses in history.

BESSENT WARNS IRAN'S OUTSIDE BACKERS TO 'STAY AWAY' AS TRUMP SEEKS TO 'ASPHYXIATE' TEHRAN'S ECONOMY

The administration had increasingly turned to economic pressure after months of military strikes failed to force Tehran into an agreement. Washington reimposed its naval blockade in July after an interim deal collapsed, and Secretary of State Marco Rubio said in September that the price Iran would pay going forward would be primarily economic, although military action remained an option.

But neither the military campaign nor the mounting economic pressure has produced a broader agreement. Iran continues to demand sanctions relief, access to frozen assets and an end to the blockade, while Washington is pressing Tehran for concessions on its nuclear program and regional activities.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent speaks with reporters outside the White House, Wednesday, in Washington, D.C. (AP Photo/Evan Vucci)

Despite months of escalating pressure, Treasury is still identifying overseas financial networks that have allowed Iran to conduct business outside traditional banking channels.

Iran has spent decades developing ways around American financial restrictions, relying on middlemen, shell companies and foreign currency exchanges to keep trade flowing even as Washington moves to cut off its access to international markets.

Monday's announcement detailed some of those channels, including exchange houses and trading companies operating across Iran, the United Arab Emirates, Turkey and Hong Kong.

Among the newly sanctioned entities was an Iranian currency exchange center that Treasury says facilitates tens of billions of dollars in annual trade, connecting exporters holding foreign currency with importers seeking to purchase goods abroad.

#5792. It’s Time to Settle With Russia

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MICHAEL C. DESCH is Packey J. Dee Professor of International Relations at the University of Notre Dame and founding Brian and Jeannelle Brady Family Director of the O’Brien Notre Dame International Security Center.

What America Should Give to Make Peace in Ukraine

In October, U.S. President Donald Trump sold his eleventh-hour deal to secure diesel from Russian President Vladimir Putin—pausing sanctions that his own administration had imposed—as a boon for the negotiations to end the war in Ukraine. The arrangement, he wrote on Truth Social, was intended to keep Russia at “the bargaining table.” Of course, it was always obvious to observers that the gambit had as much or more to do with bolstering the U.S. economy and stemming a massive victory for the Democrats in this month’s midterm elections, and in that, it failed.

Cynics have grounds for questioning all of Trump’s moves when it comes to Russia and Ukraine. He clearly dislikes Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky and shows a marked preference for illiberal leaders, especially those willing to give him easy wins. Still, a stopped clock can be right twice a day. And concessions such as the fuel deal are exactly what might move Russia and Ukraine closer to peace, even if they are not wholly motivated by public-spiritedness.

Ukraine and its backers in Europe and the United States have long maintained that Russia only responds to sticks. The problem with their argument is that since 2014, and especially since 2022, Moscow has been subjected to nothing but sticks. According to Kaja Kallas, the European Union’s foreign policy chief, the more than 25,000 sanctions imposed on Russia after its full-scale invasion of Ukraine had cost its economy almost $1.5 trillion as of June. And although Trump has backed Ukraine less consistently than U.S. President Joe Biden did, especially in his rhetoric, his administration has still provided significant financial, military, and intelligence support; his CIA has been more aggressive than Biden’s in assisting Ukraine’s offensive and defensive air operations. All of these coercive measures have proved insufficient to compel a change in Putin’s policy or turn the tide in Ukraine’s favor on the battlefield.

European leaders may fret that Trump, now a lame duck and focused on his legacy, will push for a deal on Ukraine that suits the Kremlin’s interests more than their own. But ultimately, this remains a war in which Russia has the advantage. Europe should recognize that its pressure tactics have not worked and welcome the U.S. president’s willingness to throw in a few carrots among the many sticks used to try to beat the Kremlin into submission. Washington, meanwhile, should continue to pursue a concessionary strategy by encouraging Ukraine to surrender territory, freezing NATO’s expansion, and offering sanctions relief to Moscow. Putin has shown a willingness to sign favorable deals in the past, including the Minsk agreements in 2014 and 2015, which promised an end to the civil conflict in Donbas only to collapse under the duress of Kyiv and European capitals. A more enduring peace is desperately needed in Ukraine, however unfair the price may seem.

Ukraine has had some recent success on the battlefield. Early in 2026, Kyiv launched offensives in western Zaporizhzhia that erased a year of Russian gains there. During the summer, the Ukrainian military embarked on a major counteroffensive, Operation Vivaldi, that made significant progress near Liman, delaying Russia’s campaign to take the Donbas fortress cities of Kramatorsk and Sloviansk. And after four years, Ukrainian forces have finally inflicted serious, albeit not irreparable, damage to the bridge connecting Crimea and Russia, disrupting a military transport route and tarnishing a potent symbol of Putin-era expansionism. Kyiv has also brought the war home to segments of the Russian public with its attacks on oil infrastructure and commercial targets such as warehouses for Wildberries, Russia’s largest online retailer. Supporters in Western capitals have trumpeted these achievements as evidence that Ukraine has turned the tide in a war that previously seemed to be moving in Moscow’s favor.

But tactical wins do not necessarily add up to strategic victory. And, as Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby reminded the Ukraine Defense Contact Group at a meeting earlier this month, the war will be decided on the ground. Casualties remain a huge problem for Ukraine. The few times Zelensky has addressed them, he has offered implausibly low figures for his side and cited astronomically high losses on the other. A recent study by Alexander Grable, a King’s College London war studies researcher, dissects these figures and concludes that Ukraine is suffering far higher casualties than Russia in absolute numbers—findings that, if true, refute the canard that Russia is uniquely careless with the lives of its soldiers. These days, while Ukraine pursues costly offensives to temporarily recover territory, Russia is taking cities and densely fortified areas by encirclement rather than frontal assault, reducing the risk to its fighters.

Although Ukraine’s strikes on Russian oil infrastructure have disrupted refining and strained domestic fuel supplies, Moscow has managed to cushion the blow through emergency measures and rapid repairs. In May, the U.S. Defense Intelligence Agency concluded that Kyiv’s long-range drone attacks did not “substantially affect Russian energy production,” which may explain why the Russian civilian population has not felt the full bite of them. The average Russian seems to be weathering this pain with something between resigned stoicism and increased determination to see the war through to victory. If Ukraine’s deep strikes were designed to encourage the United States to support Zelensky’s proposal for an energy truce, its effects have been counterproductive: higher oil prices have refilled the Kremlin’s war chest and greater energy costs are undermining allied support for Ukraine, especially in the United States.

Many Western battlefield analyses suggest Russia has recently lost a bit of ground. In fact, the battle lines in Ukraine remain ambiguous. According to the widely followed Military Summary Telegram channel, which applies a nuanced metric of territorial control to geolocated videos from Russian and Ukrainian sources, Russia has continued to make inroads through October and November. In the north, its troops have carved out a buffer zone on the border, making gains around Sumy and Velykyi Burluk. The see-saw battle for Kupyiansk—a fiercely contested gateway to Kharkiv and northeastern Ukraine—once again favors Moscow. In Donetsk, Russian forces have largely taken the city of Dobropillia, which could enable them to creep north behind the Donbas fortress cities and encircle them. And Russia continues to gain ground in eastern Zaporizhzhia, where its units are now edging closer to the region’s administrative capital. Operation Vivaldi may have slowed the Russian advance north of Kramatorsk, but only at a significant cost to some of Ukraine’s best units. Much like the Kursk campaign in 2024, the counteroffensive seems more like a temporary media victory than a real change in the ground war.

In the air, Russia has commenced a deep strike campaign across Ukraine, using drones and ballistic missiles to turn the tables in key regions. Russian forces have severely disrupted Kyiv’s Black Sea operations, intensified strikes against data centers and electricity infrastructure, attacked locomotives, and hit targets ever closer to Ukraine’s western borders, and are now going after major bridges across the Dnieper, which could literally cut the country in two. Russia’s quantitative and qualitative advantage in jet-powered drones allows these weapons to function as effectively as cruise missiles, and recent air operations have been more sustained and systematic as a result. Ukraine’s dearth of American-made PAC-3 interceptor missiles—a result of the United States’ own dwindling defense stockpiles—means that Russian ballistic missiles are mostly unstoppable.

In October, Trump angrily wrote on social media that Zelensky “doesn’t want to end this war, and probably never did.” This was unfair: undoubtedly, Zelensky does want to end it, just on his own terms. The aid and support Europe and the United States have lavished on Ukraine—initially necessary for preventing a catastrophic defeat—now gives its president little incentive to make peace any other way. Early in the current war, when Ukraine was unexpectedly prevailing on the battlefield, it passed on two promising peace tracks that might have ended the fighting soon after it began.

But negotiations are now the only way forward for Ukraine, and they will not progress by targeting Russia exclusively with sticks. To overcome the current impasse, Ukraine and its supporters have to put their chips on the table. That means forgoing any hope of NATO accession—or of a sustained NATO military presence in Ukraine—and relinquishing territory to Russia. If Kyiv remains unwilling to make the concessions demanded of losing parties in war, then Washington can take steps to encourage better judgement. The United States could taper down or cut off certain services and munitions it provides the Ukrainian armed forces, including Starlink and Starshield satellite connectivity, Patriot missile defense systems, and intelligence support for air defense and deep strikes on Russian targets. Any of these deprivations would be felt immediately on the battlefield.

Washington should offer carrots of its own accord, too. President Trump can lift some sanctions, such as those banning Russian commercial flights from U.S. airspace, without much controversy. Lifting others, such as those restricting fossil fuel exports and financial transactions, will be less palatable but more attractive to Russia and thus more effective. Russia’s readmission to the SWIFT interbank messaging service and other global commercial networks must be pursued accordingly.

Pro-Ukrainian hardliners argue that no amount of carrots will be enough to satisfy Putin’s voracious appetite for territory. They warn that if Russia is not stopped in Ukraine, it will continue its campaign of conquest farther west through Poland, Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia, and beyond, with no end in sight. As Zelensky warned European leaders at the Berlin Security Conference in early November, the Russian military buildup is not just directed at his country, “it is directed against you.” But although Russia is making grinding progress on the fronts in eastern Ukraine, at the rate of its advances in 2025 and this year, it would take three decades just to capture the rest of Ukraine east of the Dnieper. And the idea that Russia, with a military of under 1.6 million men and women in uniform, could defeat a NATO alliance boasting 3.65 million soldiers is implausible, especially considering NATO’s overwhelming advantages in major weapons and military spending.

Even if Russia could take on NATO directly, it is far from clear that Putin wants to. The Kremlin’s war aims in Ukraine may not seem just, but they are narrower than many analysts allege. Since his 2007 speech in Munich excoriating the United States’ global hegemony—and after the 2008 NATO summit declaration that welcomed eventual membership for Georgia and Ukraine—NATO expansion has been Putin’s idée fixe. He has been similarly preoccupied with the status of the pro-Russia citizens of Eastern Ukraine and devoted substantial effort to ensuring protection of their linguistic rights and political influence through the Minsk agreements. Only when that failed did Moscow recognize the two regions of the Donbas, Luhansk and Donetsk as independent states eligible for membership in the Russian Federation, paving the way for direct Russian intervention in the region. Nonetheless, Putin continued to pursue a negotiated solution to the conflict through Turkish President Recep Tayyip Erdogan and Israel’s then prime minister Naftali Bennett. As with the Minsk process, it was Ukraine and its backers who walked away from these negotiations when their aims—the complete liberation of all Russian-occupied territory—still appeared achievable on the battlefield.

In a round of U.S.-led peace talks earlier this month in Abu Dhabi, Russia made maximalist demands, asking for the pullback of NATO forces from Poland and the Baltic states, limits on European nuclear deterrence, and restrictions on the size and capabilities of Ukraine’s military. But read in the broader context of Putin’s singular and consistent fixations, these demands look more like negotiating ploys than dealbreakers. It has long been clear what the Kremlin will settle for: control over Ukraine’s eastern border regions and a largely demilitarized Kyiv. Whatever the ultimate terms of this deal, some basic facts are clear. There is no going back to a Ukrainian Donbas, nor a Ukrainian Crimea or Novorossiya. And whatever security guarantees Europe and the United States give Ukraine in the future, they cannot include NATO membership.

The status quo in Ukraine is unsustainable. Four years of war have lulled many of Kyiv’s supporters into complacency about the risk that continued pressure on Russia could create an escalatory spiral. There is increasingly cavalier talk in some newer NATO capitals, for example, about undertaking aggressive NATO exercises near Kaliningrad and overcoming the city’s air and missile defenses. But those steps may only further widen and deepen the war. The clear and present danger of vertical and horizontal escalation must not be dismissed.

Cracks are already showing in the West’s united front against Russia. France and other western European states are experiencing heightened social and political unrest, fueled in part by the costs of the war. In late October, Finnish President Alexander Stubb and Norwegian Prime Minister Jonas Gahr Store made a rare phone call to Putin to “convey messages directly regarding future negotiations” toward peace, according to a statement released by the two leaders. In the United States, a once bipartisan hard-line toward Russia is starting to fall out of favor, even among Democrats. In mid-October, Democratic Representative Jim Himes told the Warsaw Security Forum that a future Democratic president will have to acknowledge the “tectonic fact” that the United States can no longer afford to play its historic leading role in confronting Russia.

There is reason to think that the Trump administration’s more conciliatory approach has already opened the door to diplomacy. The Oleshky truce brokered by First Lady Melania Trump and the Vatican successfully evacuated civilians trapped in a Russian-occupied city south of Kherson. And the president’s comments in his 60 Minutes interview this month suggested that he is ready to ply Russia with the incentives necessary to get a deal over the line. These are steps in the right direction, but in addition to altering the balance of carrots offered to Russia, the Trump administration must consider providing Ukraine security guarantees outside of the NATO framework.

Now in the twilight of his political career, Trump has made clear his desire to leave a legacy in the form of Nobel Peace Prizes and triumphal arches. Ending a major war in Ukraine would be a crowning achievement, which is why he has waxed lyrical about a “big, beautiful agreement” since he entered his second term in office. And if Trump still believes that China is the pacing threat of the twenty-first century, reintegrating Russia into the global economy and weakening the axis between Putin and Chinese leader Xi Jinping will be paramount for advancing his long-term political vision.

No one has lost money betting against peace in Ukraine since 2014, and it would be foolhardy to expect Trump to pursue diplomacy solely for altruistic reasons. But perhaps if he sees peace as being in his own interest, as well as that of the United States, he might, despite himself, broker an end to a bloody and increasingly fruitless war. Doing so could be one of the few positive legacies of his second term, even if it does not get him to Oslo.

#5791. Former Navy SEAL says New Jersey’s Islamophobia bill puts protected speech in the crosshairs

Bill Brown, a Parlatore Law Group partner and former Navy SEAL, tells Fox News Digital why he believes New Jersey’s Islamophobia bill could make people think twice before criticizing religious and political ideas.

A New Jersey proposal aimed at combating anti-Muslim discrimination could make Americans think twice before criticizing religious and political ideas, a lawyer and former Navy SEAL warned.

Bill Brown, a partner at Parlatore Law Group, told Fox News Digital that Assembly Bill 1146 contains language broad enough to discourage public debate, even though the legislation expressly preserves First Amendment rights.

"The mere threat of state scrutiny is a direct assault on public debate," Brown said.

TRUMP DOJ EXPANDS RELIGIOUS LIBERTY PROTECTIONS AFTER MAJOR SUPREME COURT RULINGS

A New Jersey bill would create a state definition of Islamophobia for officials to consider in certain civil and criminal matters. An unrelated photo shows Muslim men praying in Marseille, France.

The proposal would establish a state definition of Islamophobia and require public officials and law enforcement officers to consider it when determining whether alleged discriminatory acts were motivated by anti-Muslim bias.

Brown argued that requiring officials to use a definition that includes harsh criticism and other speech could make people afraid to speak openly.

"The First Amendment guarantees that no religion or theology is immune to criticism," he said.

Democratic Assemblyman Joe Danielsen sponsors the bill, which is co-sponsored by fellow Democrats Assemblywomen Verlina Reynolds-Jackson and Mitchelle Drulis and Assemblyman Roy Freiman. The legislation describes a uniform definition as a tool to help officials identify and investigate anti-Muslim crime, harassment and discrimination."

Fox News Digital made multiple attempts to contact Danielsen and all three co-sponsors for comment but did not receive a response.

Its definition includes "hatred or severe disparagement towards Muslim persons or their culture," as well as "rhetorical, violent, or physical anti-Muslim manifestations."

Other examples include pulling hijabs from Muslim women, discriminatory treatment and blaming Muslims collectively for wrongdoing committed by an individual or group.

CONNECTICUT BARS NATIVITY DISPLAY IN STATE CAPITOL BECAUSE IT'S 'RELIGIOUS,' LAWSUIT THREAT SAYS

The bill would create no standalone criminal offense for criticizing Islam. It directs officials to consider the definition when reviewing an alleged violation of an existing policy, law or regulation prohibiting discriminatory acts.

It also explicitly says nothing in the legislation should diminish or infringe on rights protected by the First Amendment or New Jersey’s Constitution.

AMERICA'S FIRST MUSLIM SENATOR EXPOSES A POLITICAL BLIND SPOT

Asked about that safeguard, Brown maintained that the prospect of scrutiny itself could discourage people from speaking, regardless of whether a case ultimately succeeds.

He also singled out a provision in the bill’s legislative findings that lists "anti-Islamism" among terms that may describe the same characteristics as Islamophobia. Brown argued that the wording risks blurring the distinction between prejudice against Muslim people and criticism of a political movement.

Former Navy SEAL and attorney Bill Brown warns that New Jersey’s proposed Islamophobia bill could discourage Americans from criticizing religious and political ideas. (Jahi Chikwendiu/The Washington Post via Getty Images)

"Islamism is a distinct geopolitical movement," he said. "It’s not just a religious faith."

Brown described the proposal as a "backdoor blasphemy law," arguing that government should protect people’s rights while allowing their religious beliefs to face public scrutiny.

The legislation excludes criticism of Muslim-majority countries comparable to criticism directed at other countries. Brown argued that criticism of religious and political ideology also needs protection.

Brown said his concerns are informed in part by his military service. He recalled responding to two suicide bombings in Iraq and said he remains committed to speaking out against jihadist ideology.

He also recalled studying Arabic at Rutgers after leaving the military, where Muslim American classmates helped him when he struggled with the language.

"True diversity is diversity of thought," he said.
